PREFACE pt;

This Report has been prepared for submission to the Governor under

Article 151 of the Constitution.

Chapters I and II of this Report respectively contain Audit observations on
matters arising from examination of Finance Accounts and Appropriation

Accounts of the State Government for the year ended 31 March 2001.

The remaining chapters deal with the findings of performance audit and
audit of transactions in the various departments including the Public
Works and Irrigation Department, audit of Stores and Stock, Revenue
Receipts, audit of Autonomous Bodies, Statutory Corporation,
Government Companies and departmentally run commercial

undertakings.

The cases mentioned in the Report are among those which came to notice
in the course of test audit of accounts during the year
2000-01, as well as those which had come to notice in earlier years but
could not be dealt with in previous Reports; matters relating to the period

subsequent to 2000-01 have also been included wherever necessary.

xi


-


This Report contains eight chapters. Chapter-I contains a detailed analysis of the

financial position of the State. Chapter-II reviews the Government's control over
expenditure during the year. The remaining six chapters contain 8 reviews and 26
paragraphs based on audit of certain selected schemes and programmes and
financial transactions of the Government. A synopsis of the findings contained in
the reviews and important paragraphs is presented in this overview.

The assets and liabilities of the State Government grew from Rs.2729.97 crore
and Rs.1698.50 crore in 1999-2000 to Rs.3045.30 crore and Rs.2092.65 crore in
2000-01 respectively. The ratio of assets to liabilities declined from 2.38 in 1996-
97 to 1.45 in 2000-01.

During 2000-01 the Revenue receipts of the State Government were Rs.1044.62
crore against which Revenue expenditure was Rs.1123.44 crore resulting in
revenue deficit of Rs.78.82 crore.

There was revenue surplus during the period from 1996-97 to 1998-99 but the
State was having revenue deficits during 1999-2000 and 2000-01.

The fiscal deficit increased from Rs.156.95 crore in 1996-97 to Rs.226.61 crore
during 2000-01.

The interest payment increased from Rs.65.59 crore in 1996-97 to Rs.177.16
crore in 2000-01. Even though the interest payment increased substantially the
borrowed funds were not utilised optimally and kept idle in civil deposit or assets
which resulted in loss of interest of Rs.2.56 crore.

The amount of Capital expenditure decreased from Rs.363.77 crore in 1999-2000
to Rs.147.49 crore in 2000-01 and its share in total expenditure decreased from 21
to 12 per cent during the same period.

As on 31 March 2001, 5 of the Government companies in which Government had
invested Rs.10.35 crore, were running under loss. While the interest on market
borrowings during the year was 12 per cent the investment in Government
companies etc. fetched nil.

During the period from 1996-97 to 2000-01 the total liabilities of the Government
had grown by 154 per cent. This was on account of 158 per cent growth in
internal debt, 141 per cent in loans and advances from Government of India and
160 per cent growth in other liabilities.

Analysis of financial data of the Government revealed that the State Government
had negative BCRs ed the five years, suggesting that Government had to
depend only on for meeting its Plan expenditure. The ratio of Capital

[2. Appropriation Audit and Control Over | Appropriation Audit and Control Over Expenditure | |

During 2000-01 expenditure of Rs.2446.08 crore was incurred against the total
grants and appropriations of Rs.3324.99 crore resulting in saving of Rs.878.91
crore. The overall saving was the result of saving of Rs.964.68 crore in 70 cases
of grants and appropriations offset by excess of Rs.85.77 crore in 9 cases of grants
and appropriations. The excess of Rs.85.77 crore required regularisation by the
Legislature under Article 205 of the Constitution.

Supplementary provision of Rs.1515.89 crore obtained during 2000-01
constituted 84 per cent of original budget provision of Rs.1809.10 crore. In 33
cases, supplementary provision of Rs.122.88 crore proved unnecessary in view of
final saving in each case being more than supplementary provision obtained in
March 2001.

In 39 cases expenditure fell short by more than Rs.1 crore in each case and also
by more than 10 per cent or more of the total provision.

In 7 cases there were persistent savings in excess of Rs.10 lakh in each case and
20 per cent or more of the provision.

(Paragraphs 2.1 to 2.4.9)

3. Audit Reviews on Development and Welfare activities

The aim of the programme was to provide elementary education to the children in
the 6-14 age group who remained out of formal education and to bring them in the
mainstream of formal education. A review of the programme through test check
of records relating to the period from April 1995 to March 2001 revealed that
despite short-release of fund by the State Government there were huge savings
There was short-release of Central fund due to failure of the State Government to
fulfil the conditions. Implementation of the programme was affected due to
retention of money in deposit account. The unauthorised diversion of fund also
affected the basic objective of the programme. There wei
NFE centres in areas where formal education schools existed, Despite specific
guidelines there were low coverage of hilly and tribal areas. In-service training
was not imparted to NFE functionaries but the fund sanctioned for the purp ose
continued to be retained in the deposit account. Prescribed courses गा NFE

re cases of opening of

Xvi


Overview

primary level and upper primary level was not followed and thus the intention of
the scheme was frustrated.

Against Rs.3.49 crore (Central share: Rs.3.05 crore; State share: Rs.0.44 crore)
provided during 2000-01, expenditure of Rs.1.26 crore was incurred by the
Department resulting in savings of Rs.2.23 crore. Reasons for such huge savings
were not intimated by the Department.

There was short-release of State matching share of Rs.2.20 crore during 1995-96

to 2000-01.

Government of India had not released the second instalment of Rs.1.28 crore for
the years 1998-99 and 1999-2000 due to the failure of the State Government to
fulfil the conditions.

NFE funds of Rs.48.32 lakh for training and learning material etc. were lying in
Deposit Account since March 1997.

Rupees 42.94 lakh released for running of NFE centres was diverted towards
construction and repair of office rooms, chowkidar’s quarters and compound
walls, purchase of furniture, office equipment and vehicle which were not
admissible.

Relevant records for purchase and actual receipt of teaching and learning
materials and office equipment worth Rs.21.60 lakh could not be made available
to audit. In their absence authenticity of the expenditure could not be established

in audit.

Students learning requirements were not assessed and supplied from time to time.
No record of receipt and distribution of teaching and learning materials were

available.

The prescribed condensed courses of two years for primary and three years for
upper primary were not followed.

Against 100 centres required for each project, the shortfall of centres per project
during 2000-01 was 43 per cent. Against 25 students per centre, the shortfall in
enrolment was 16 per cent during 2000-01.

Strategy of testing and certification of NFE students for entering into formal
education was not done and no information of students entered into formal
education was available.

(Paragraph 3.1)

5 Follow-up action on the Memorandum o Uni

A Memorandum of Understanding (MOU) was signed (19 April 1999) between
GOI, Ministry of Finance and the Government of Manipur (GOM) regarding
measures to be adopted by GOM to deal with the situation arising out of drawal of
overdraft of Rs,95 crore as on 17 April, 1999 by the GOM. Test check of records
of the Finance Department, Government of Manipur and 40 Government
Departments/Offices, 6 Autonomous District Councils and 4 Public Sector
Undertakings/Autonomous Bodies revealed that the State Government could not
solve the problems of Cash Deficit. Actions initiated by the State Government as
regards raising of additional resources and applying of economy measures were
hot effective. Commitment made in respect of power sector could also not be
fulfilled,

Government’s proposal for writing off of Rs.12.96 crore accumulated under Other
Deposits did not materialise as the amount mostly pertained to unutilised Central
assistance refundable to Government of India.

Only 3 lawyers out of 903 lawyers registered with All Manipur Bar Association
and High Court Bar Association had paid Professional Tax during 2000-01,

Though the rates of OPD/Hospital Administration/investigation charges, fees ete.
were revised in September 2000, the collection of revenue in the Health
department decreased during 2000-01 by 34 per cent.

As against the commitment of recovery of outstanding dues of Rs.9.79 crore
agreed upon in the MOU, only Rs.43.42 lakh were recovered during 1999-2000.

Inspite of the commitment made by the State Government, the actual realisation
of power dues during 1999-2000 and 2000-01 was less than the target by 32 and
30 per cent respectively.

T&D/Commercial losses valuing Rs.4.38 crore could not be arrested by the Power
Department during 2000-01. हु
Though the power tariff was revised in March 2000,

XViii


Overview
AD. A review of the working of the department/District Blindness Control
Societies (DBCSs) revealed that due to late receipt of fund from the State
Government there was overall saving. No assessment survey was conducted by
the DBCSs to ascertain the number of blind people in the State. No programme
for rehabilitation of the incurably blind was made in the State for reasons not on
record. Operation theatres and wards attached to eye care units of medical
hospitals and district hospitals were neither renovated nor furnished as no fund
was provided either by the State Ophthalmic Cell or DBCSs from the Central
assistance. No Eye Bank was established to solve the problem of corneal
blindness. Due to shortage of trained doctors and nurses in the State,
implementation of the programme was frustrated apart from deprival of benefit to
the targeted people for getting free treatment as per programme.

Against total grants of Rs.78.61 lakh received from the Government of India
during 1996-97 to 2000-01, Rs.9.19 lakh had not been released by the State
Government to the department for implementation of the programme as of March
2001.

Against total grants of Rs.43 lakh released by the Government of India during
1996-97 to 2000-01, Rs.24.65 lakh was received by the DBCS. The discrepancy
of Rs.18.35 lakh was not reconciled with the Government of India.

The State Government appointed staff at different grades on contract basis in the
time scale of the State Government instead of consolidated pay as per norms fixed
by the Government of India for the period from December 1999 to January 2001
resulting in extra expenditure of Rs.4.36 lakh towards salary constituting more
than 10 per cent of the expenditure incurred on the programme.

Against the target of 10,000 cataract surgeries to be conducted during 1996-97 to
2000-01, only 3,244 surgeries were done which resulted in shortfall of

achievement by 67.56 per cent.

Shortfall of group meetings was 46 per cent against the targets during the period

from 1996-97 to 2000-01.
(Paragraph 3.3)

3.3 (ii) National Tube ‘ontrol Programme

Based on the findings of the committee of experts set up by the Government of
India in 1992, a revised strategy for National Tuberculosis Control Programme
(RNTPC) was introduced with emphasis on cure of infectious cases through
administration of directly observed short course of chemotherapy to all TB
diagnosed patients to achieve a cure rate of over 85 per cent. A review of the
programme revealed that Central assistance for purchase of anti TB drug released
to the State Government remained unutilised due to its non-release by the State
Government to the Director of Health Services. Non-appointment of Medical
Officer in the TB units of the State frustrated the aim of providing diagnostic and
treatment facilities to the patients. Non-retrieval of defaulters to treatment also

re

frustrated the aim of the programme to control infection. The TB patients were
deprived of the free of cost X-ray facilities due to non-availability of X-ray
machine/film. Monitoring of the programme at the State level was not fone as
there was no regular flow of information from the District TB Control Societies.

Central assistance of Rs.8.05 lakh for 2000-01 remained unutilised due to non-
release of fund by the State Government.

Non-utilisation of Anti TB drug worth Rs.5.99 lakh purchased by the DHS,
Manipur during 1999-2000 had frustrated the objective of covering the targeted
patients.

Civil work for establishment of 15 Microscopy centres in 4 districts had not been
taken up causing delay in implementation of the programme.

Detection of new cases decreased during 1997-98 to 2000-01. No survey to
identify new cases was conducted.

Cure rate of new smear positive cases during 1998-99 and 1999-2000 was 65.9
per cent as against the prescribed norms of 85 per cent.

71 Medical Officers (MOs), 26 Laboratory Technicians (LTs), 1 Senior Treatment
Supervisor (STS), 235 Multipurpose Health Supervisors (MPHSs) and 431
Multipurpose Health Workers (MPHWs) of 6 districts are yet to be trained.

Overview

Against the total receipts of Rs.12.89 crore (Government of India: Rs.9.47 crore,
unspent balance from the State Government: Rs.2.43 crore and Misc. receipts:
Rs.0.99 crore) during 1997-98 to 2000-01, actual expenditure incurred by the
MACS was Rs.12.79 crore leaving an unspent balance of Rs.0.10 crore at the end
of March 2001.

During 1998-99 to 2000-01, Rs.3.99 crore was diverted from one component to
another without approval of the NACO.

The MACS incurred irregular expenditure of Rs.60 lakh towards construction of
100-bedded extension ward at JN Hospital contrary to the guidelines of the
scheme which totally barred civil construction from out of the funds of the
programme.

Under the component of Priority Targeted Intervention for Groups at High Risk,
an expenditure of Rs.19.91 lakh was incurred in excess of admissible norms.

The actual attendance in the Family Health Awareness Campaigns held between
April 1999 and April 2001 was 5,24,536 only, against the targeted population of
27,25,283 indicating shortfall of 81 per cent. The expenditure, however, exceeded
by Rs.1.06 crore (272 per cent) over the approved norms of NACO.

During 1997-98 to 2000-01 the overall shortfalls in achievement in respect of
training of doctors, nurses and paramedics were 58, 71 and 79 per cent
respectively against the targets.

(Paragraph 3.5)

The objective of the programme was elimination of Leprosy by the end of 2000
AD by reducing the case load to less than 1 per 10,000 population. The
Government of India (GOI) provided cent per cent financial assistance to State
Government for implementation of the programme. GOI also provided cash
assistance direct to the 8 District Leprosy Societies constituted to assist in
implementing the programme. Although 8 District Leprosy Units (one each in 8
districts) established prior to 1996-97 were functioning, no Leprosy
Rehabilitation-cum-Promotion Unit and Regional Leprosy Training & Referral
Institution were established in the State so far. Reconstructive Survey Unit to
provide major or minor surgical facilities to the patients cured but deformed, was
not set up due to non-finalisation of accommodation. There were cases of
deformity due to failure of early detection and prompt treatment of patients which
frustrated the basic objective-of the programme.

Central assistance of Rs.7.59 lakh was not utilised by the end of March 2001 due
to ban imposed by RBI (Rs.5.57 lakh) and non-release of fund by the State
Government (Rs.1.00 lakh).

3.4 Accelerated Rural Water Supply Programme (ARWSP) and
_ Accelerated Urban Water Supply Programme (AUWSP)

A ARWSP

The Public Health Engineering did not carry out a complete survey at any point of
time so as to detect the problem habitations. Water sources were identified by
conducting physical survey using topographical maps but no satellite images and
hydrological maps were used. Except for testing the quality of water, no other
factors such as sustainability of the sources were taken into account. There were
29 cases of source depletion during 1997-2000. Non-Government organisations or
any other agencies were not involved in implementing the programme. Due to
inadequate release of fund by the State Government there was shortfall in
coverage of problem habitations and short-procurement of various construction
material which affected the execution of the schemes. The department had not
taken up any programme of extension of drinking water facilities to schools and
anganwadis as required under the scheme. Inventory of the created assets were
not prepared at the departmental level and there were cases of theft of polypipes
and GI pipes due to negligence and irresponsibility of the user community.

Against fund of Rs.36.35 crore provided by the State

The schemes were taken up without conducting any survey so as to ascertain

‘Another 5 schemes scheduled for completion between March 2000 and March

approval of the schemes by the State Government causing delay in
commencement of works ranging from one to five years and as a result the
department was losing an anticipated revenue of Rs.53.35 lakh annually.

3.6 Audit of Environmental Acts And Rules relating to Air
Pollution and Waste Management

In March 1996 the State was declared as an air pollution control area. The major
sources of air pollution in the State are smoke emission from unmaintained
vehicles, use of adulterated vehicle fuel, open burning of Urban solid wastes,
Stone crushing units, Brick fields etc. Though the Manipur Pollution Control
Board (NPCB) granted consent to 183 industrial units during 1996-2001 for
operating plants in the State it did not prescribe/approve pollution control
equipment for installation and operation in the premises of the industry. The
Board also did not conduct inspections at regular intervals in the industrial units
and conduct impact analysis after the consented units had gone into productive
operation. Carbon monoxide levels in the State were also not assessed by the
Board due to non-procurement of the equipment.

Of 66,115 registered vehicles in Imphal city, automobile emission test conducted
in respect of 3,891 vehicles only as of March 2000 which was totally inadequate
to prevent, control and abate air pollution. These tests had never been conducted
in the other districts of the State due to paucity of funds.

The Imphal Municipal Council unauthorisedly dumped wastes in plots not
protected by boundary walls and guarded against, trespassers. The dumping was
disorderly and exposed to the atmosphere spreading obnoxious smell in the
surrounding areas. No survey was conducted by the Board to assess the extent of
adverse effects on the health and environment caused due to unsystematic

disposal for wastes.

(Paragraph 6.6)

al
The conditions for plying of motor vehicles and levy and collection of taxes
thereon are governed by various Acts and Rules which empower the Transport
Department to levy and collect road tax, registration fee, inspection fee, permit
fee, composite fee etc. Though there has been ample scope in these Acts and
Rules for enhancing revenue from taxes on motor vehicles, the collection of
revenue has shown a fluctuating trend due to improper monitoring, non-
maintenance of records in proper formats and lack of weigh bridge. Review of
taxes on vehicles relating to the period from 1995-96 to 2000-01 revealed the

following—

Though there has been a steady growth of registration of vehicles during 1996 97
to 2000-01, the realisation of taxes/revenues therefrom has shown an erratic trend

The Department failed to impose penalty of Rs.15.17 lakh on vehicles which
violated provisions of the Acts and Rules.

(Paragraph 7.5)

3.8 Rural Electrification schemes |

Rural Electrification (RE) schemes were being implemented in the State as State
Plan schemes since 1969-70. The schemes were brought under the purview of the
Rural Electrification Corporation of India (REC) from 1977-78. Since then, the
schemes are funded concurrently under State Plan and through loan assistance
from the REC. The main objective of the schemes is to provide socio-economic
benefit to the rural population by electrification of villages and intensification
thereof. Following are the important findings noticed during review of the scheme
relating to the period from 1995-96 to 2000-01.

The State Government paid (1999-2000) penal interest of Rs.1.05 crore due to
non-repayment of principal and interest on the REC loan on due dates.

There were shortfalls ranging between 27 and 89 per cent in electrifying villages
during 1995-96 to 1999-2000. No villages were electrified during 2000-2001.

Extremely low achievement of service connections (only 7 per cent) during 1995-
96 to 2000-01 negated the economic viability of the RE schemes.

Non-completion of RE works in 227 villages resulted in locking up of borrowed
funds amounting to Rs.25.47 crore with consequential interest liability of Rs.5.13
crore.

There was diversion of materials worth Rs.98.96 lakh from the RE schemes to
maintenance works.

There was loss of Rs.1.21 crore due to theft of materials from RE works.

The Department proposed closure of 26 schemes (sanctioned between February
1986 and March 1991) in June 1997 and failed to avail sanctioned loan of

Rs.5.5|crore against them. As a result only 475 villages were electrified against
the target of 613.

Due to slow progress/non-execution of special category schemes there was

locking up of Rs.9.74 crore resulting in fruitless liability of interest of Rs.1.31
crore per annum.

General view of Government Companies and Statutory Corporation

As on 31 March 2001 there were 15 Government Companies (13 working
Companies and 2 non-working Companies) and one working Statutory
Corporation in the State. The total investment of working Public Sector
Undertakings was Rs.100.02 crore (working Government Companies Rs.69.32
crore and working Statutory Corporation Rs.30.70 crore).

The total investment in 2 non-working Companies was Rs.1.15 crore as on 31
March 2001.

Out of 2 non-working Government Companies one Company was under
liquidation for 2 years and substantial investment of Rs.0.42 crore was involved in
this Company.

The accounts of 13 working Companies and one working Corporation were in
arrears ranging from 4 to 19 years.

Of the 5 loss incurring working companies, aggregate loss incurred by 3 working
Companies was Rs.4.33 crore and that by the working Corporation was Rs.16.70
crore.

No dividend was declared by any of the 4 profit making working Companies.

This chapter discusses the financial position of the State Government, based on
the analysis of the information contained in the Finance Accounts. The analysis is
based on the trends in the receipts and expenditure, the quality of expenditure and
the financial management of the State Government. In addition, the chapter also
contains a section on the analysis of indicators of financial performance of the
Government, based on certain ratios and indices developed on the basis of the
information contained in the Finance Accounts and other information furnished
by the State Government. Some of the terms used in this chapter are described in
the Appendix I.

In the Government accounting system comprehensive accounting of the fixed
assets like land and buildings etc., owned by the Government is not done.
However, the Government accounts do capture the financial liabilities of the
Government and the assets created out of the expenditure incurred by the
Government. Following table gives an abstract of such liabilities and the assets as
on 31 March 2001, compared with the corresponding position on 31 March 2000.
While the liabilities in this statement consists mainly of internal borrowings, loans
and advances from the Government of India, receipts from the Public Account
and Reserve Funds, the assets comprise mainly the capital outlay, loans and
advances given by the State Government and the cash balances. It would be seen
from the table that while the liabilities grew by 23 per cent, the assets grew by 12
per cent during 2000-01. This reflects a relatively better financial position of the
Government as compared to the previous year when the assets marginally
decreased by 0.36 per cent against a growth of 19 per cent in liabilities.

1. The abridged accounts in the above table have to be read with comments and
explanations in the Finance Accounts.

accounts, as shown in the above table indicates the position on cash basis, as
opposed to accrual basis in commercial accounting. Consequently, items

1.3.2. It would be seen that the revenue receipts constitute the most significant
source of funds for the State Government. Their relative share went up from 62.40
per cent in 1999-2000 to 64.01 per cent in 2000-01. The receipts from Public
Debt also went up from Rs.90.11 crore to Rs.191.86 crore. Against net receipt of
Rs.393.86 crore from Public Account during 1999-2000, there was net receipt of
Rs.271.15 crore in 2000-01. This was mainly due to lesser growth in small
savings and suspense and miscellaneous transactions during the year.

3



uit Report forthe 3a nd NI Report for the year ended 31 March 2001

1.3.3. The funds were mainly applied for revenue expenditure and capital
expenditure. The percentage of its application to revenue expenditure and capitay
expenditure during 2000-01 went down from 99.85 to 77.88 per cent and lending
for development purposes from 0.15 to 0.05 per cent as compared to the previous

year.

1.4.1. Table No.1.3 gives the details of the receipts and disbursements made by
the State Government. The Revenue receipts (Rs.1044.62 crore) during the year
were less than the Revenue expenditure (Rs.1 123.44 crore) resulting in revenue
deficit of Rs.78.82 crore. The Revenue receipts comprised Tax revenue (Rs.49.07
crore), Non-tax revenue (Rs.41.66 crore), State’s share of Union taxes and duties
(Rs.163.52 crore) and grants-in-aid from the Central Government (Rs.790.37
crore). The main sources of Tax revenue were sales tax (64 per cent). Non-tax
revenue came mainly from Economic Services (68 per cent).

1.4.2. Against receipts of Rs.0.52 crore from recoveries of loans and advances
and Rs.417.07 crore from public debt, the expenditure was Rs.147.49 crore on
capital outlay, Rs.0.82 crore on disbursement of loans and advances and
Rs.101.55 crore on repayment of public debt. The receipts in the Public Account
amounted to Rs.963.47 crore, against which the disbursements of Rs.692.32 crore
were made. The State Government resorted to overdraft from the Reserve Bank of
India and the closing overdraft at the end of the year stood at Rs.400.50 crore. The
net effect of the transactions in the Consolidated Fund and Public Account was an
increase in the cash balance of Rs.360.06 crore from negative balance of
Rs.113.93 crore at the beginning of the year to Rs.246.13 crore at the end of the
year.

and expenditure are discussed in the following paragraphs, with reference to the
information contained in Table No. 1.3 and 1.4.

Non-tax revenue

1.5.3 The Non-tax revenue constituted 4 per cent of the Revenue receipts of y
Government and their share in the Revenue receipts declined gradually from 6 be
cent in 1996-97 to 4 per cent in 2000-01, Its share in Revenue receipts remainip
the same as in the previous year, realisation of Non-tax revenue decreased by 0.9%
crore during 2000-01 over the previous year.

1.5.4 The State’s share of Union taxes (excise duty and income tax) decreaseg
by 29 per cent, while the grants-in-aid from the Central Government increased |,
56 per cent during the five years period. But the State share of Union Tax,
decreased by Rs.154.35 crore as compared to the previous year. The total receipts
from the Government of India during 2000-01 represented 91 per cent of the tota
Revenue receipts of the Government.

[1.6 Revenue expenditure) Revenue expenditure

1.6.1. The Revenue expenditure represented 108 per cent of the total Revenue
receipts of the State Government and increased from Rs.710.30 crore in 1996-97
to Rs.1123.44 crore in 2000-01, representing an increase of 58 per cent. However,
the expenditure during 2000-01 decreased by 17 per cent over the previous year,
The Non-Plan revenue expenditure during the year decreased by Rs.154.45 crore
(14 per cent) as compared to previous year while Plan Revenue expenditure
declined/decreased by Rs.70.10 crore (27 per cent) during the year in comparison
to the previous year. However, over the five year period ending 2000-01, the
expenditure under plan increased by Rs.15.84 crore (9 per cent) while that under
Non-Plan increased by Rs.397.30 crore (74 per cent). A comparison shows that
the rate of growth in Non-Plan component of Revenue expenditure surpassed the
Plan expenditure as can be seen in Chart 1.2.

Chart 1.2
Growth of Plan and Non-Plan revenue expenditure (Rs. in Crore)

1.6.2 Sector-wise analysis shows that while the expenditure on General Services
increased by 113 per cent, from Rs.242.13 crore in 1996-97 to Rs.514.82 crore in
2000-01, the corresponding increase in expenditure under Social Services was

10


=e An Overview of the Finances of the State Government

45 per cent and that under Economic Services was only 9 per cent during the
same period. As a proportion of total expenditure, the share of General Services
increased from 25 per cent in 1996-97 to 40 per cent in 2000-01 whereas it
increased from 28 to 31 per cent under Social Services and declined from 20 to 17
per cent under Economic Services during the same period.

Interest payments

1.6.3 Interest payments increased by 170 per cent from Rs.65.59 crore in 1996-
97 to Rs.177.16 crore in 2000-01. This is further discussed in the section on
financial indicators. Even though interest payment increased substantially the
borrowed funds were not utilised optimally and were kept idle in civil deposit or
assets as brought out in paragraph numbers 2.5, 3.1.14, 3.4.11, 3.5.6, 3.5.26, 3.11,
3.12, 3.15, 4.3, 5.1 and 5.2. This resulted in loss of interest of Rs.2.56 crore
calculated at the average of the borrowing rates applicable to the periods such
funds were kept idle.

Chapter-I_An Overview of the Finances of the State Government

Capital expenditure leads to asset creation. In addition, financial assets arise from
Moneys invested in institutions or undertakings outside Government ie. Public
Sector Undertakings (PSUs), corporations etc. and loans and advances. During
2000-01 the capital expenditure has decreased by 59 per cent as compared to
previous year. Also, its share in total expenditure has decreased from 27 per cent
in 1996-97 to 12 per cent in 2000-01. Table number 1.4 shows that most of the
capital expenditure during the year has been on Economic Services and Social
Services and on the Plan side.

1.8 — Quality of Expenditure ४
1.8.1 Government spends money for different activities ranging from
maintenance of law and order and regulatory functions to various developmental
activities. Government expenditure is broadly classified into Plan and Non-plan
and revenue and capital. While the Plan and Capital expenditure are usually
associated with asset creation, the Non-Plan and Revenue expenditure are
identified with expenditure on establishment, maintenance and services. By
definition, therefore, in general the Plan and Capital expenditure can be viewed as
contributing to the quality of expenditure.

1.8.2 Wastage in public expenditure, diversions of funds and funds blocked in
incomplete projects would also impinge negatively on the quality of expenditure.
Similarly, funds transferred to deposit heads in the Pubic Account, after booking
them as expenditure, can also be considered as a negative factor in judging the
quality of expenditure. As the expenditure was not actually incurred in the
concemed year it should be excluded from the figures of expenditure for that year.
Another possible indicator is the increase in the expenditure on General Services,
to the detriment of Economic and Social Services.

Ways and means advances and overdraft

1.9.5 Under an agreement with the Reserve Bank of India, the State
Government had to maintain with the Bank a minimum daily cash balance of
Rs.0.24 crore. If the balance fell below the agreed minimum on any day, the
deficiency had to be made good by taking ways and means advances
(WMA)/Overdraft (OD) from the Bank. In addition, special ways and means
advances are also made by the Bank whenever necessary. Recourse to WMA/OD
means a mismatch between the receipts and expenditure of the Government, and
hence reflects poor on the financial management in Government.

1.9.6 The position of ways and means advances/overdraft taken by the State
Government and interest paid thereon during 1996-97 to 2000-2001 is detailed
below—

1.9.7 The position indicates poor cash management by the State Government
leading to drawal of huge amount of ways and means advances and overdrafts and
consequent payment of interest thereon. The annual interest liability has increased
from Rs.0.61 crore in 1996-97 to Rs.7.76 crore in 2000-01.

Deficit

1.9.8 Deficits in Government account represent gaps between the receipts and
expenditure. The nature of deficit is an important indicator of the prudence of
financial management in the Government. Further, the ways of financing the
deficit and the application of the funds raised in this manner are important
pointers of the fiscal prudence of the Government. The discussion in this section
relates to three concepts of deficit viz., Revenue Deficit, Fiscal Deficit and
Primary Deficit.

1.9.9 The Revenue Deficit is the excess of revenue expenditure over revenue
receipts. The Fiscal Deficit may be defined as the excess of revenue and capital
expenditure (including net loans given) over the revenue receipts (including
grants-in-aid received). Primary Deficit is fiscal deficit less interest payments.
The following table gives a break-up of the deficit in Government account.

Application of the borrowed funds (Fiscal Deficit)

1.9.11 The fiscal deficit represents total net borrowings of the Government.
These borrowings are applied for meeting the Capital Expenditure (CE) and for
giving loans to various bodies for developmental and other purposes. The relative
proportions of these applications would indicate the financial prudence of the
State Government and also the sustainability of its operations because continued
borrowing for revenue expenditure would not be sustainable in the long run.

corporations, Government companies and co-operative institutions efc., and
payment of interest and dividend by them. They constitute contingent liability of

Considering that the outstanding debt has been increasing year after year the net
availability of funds through public borrowings is going to reduce further.

1.11.1 A Government may either wish to maintain its existing level of activity or
increase its level of activity. For maintaining its current level of activity it would
be necessary to know how far the means of financing are sustainable. Similarly, if
Government wished to increase its level of activity it would be pertinent to
examine the flexibility of the means of financing. Finally, Government's increased
vulnerability in the process. All the State Governments continue to increase the
level of their activity principally through Five Year Plans which translate to
Annual development plans and are provided for in the State Budget. Broadly, it
can be stated that Non-Plan expenditure represents Government maintaining the
existing level of activity”, while Plan expenditure entails expansion of activity.
Both these activities require resource mobilisation increasing Government’s
vulnerability. In short, financial health of a Government can be described in terms
of sustainability, flexibility and vulnerability. These terms are defined as

follows—

(9 Sustainability

Sustainability is the degree to which a Government can maintain existing

programmes and meet existing creditor re

burden

ee ५० >>

quirements without increasing the debt

Chapter-1_An Overview of the Finances of the State Government
(ii) Flexibility
Flexibility is the degree to which a Government can increase its financial

Tesources to respond to rising commitments by either expending its revenues or
increasing its debt burden.

(iii) Vulnerability

Vulnerability is the degree to which a Government becomes dependent on and
therefore vulnerable to sources of funding outside its control or influence, both
domestic and international.

(iv) — Transparency

There is also the issue of financial information provided by the Government. This
consists of Annual Financial Statement (Budget) and the Accounts. As regards the
budget the important parameters are timely presentation indicating the efficiency
of budgetary process and the accuracy of the estimates. As regards, accounts
timeliness in submission, for which milestones exist and completeness of
accounts would be the principal criteria.

1.11.2 Information available in Finance Accounts can be used to flush out
Sustainability, Flexibility and Vulnerability that can be expressed in terms of
certain indices/ratios worked out from the Finance Accounts. The list of such
indices/ratios is given in the Appendix I B. Table No.1.15 indicates the behaviour
of these indices/ratios over the period from 1996-97 to 2000-01. The implications
of these indices/ratios for the state of the financial health of the State Government
are discussed in the following paragraphs.

1.11.3 The behaviour of the indices/ ratios is discussed below—

(i) Balance from current revenues (BCR)

BCR is defined as revenue receipts minus Plan assistance grants minus non-Plan
revenue expenditure. A positive BCR shows that the State Government has
surplus from its revenues for meeting Plan expenditure. The table shows that the
State Government has negative BCRs in all the five years, suggesting that
Government had to depend only on borrowings for meeting its Plan expenditure.

(ii) Interest ratio

The higher the ratio the lesser the ability of the Government to service any fresh
debt and meet its revenue expenditure from its revenue receipts. In case of
Manipur the ratio has moved significantly from 0.07 in 1996-97 to 0.17 in 2000-
01. A rising interest ratio has adverse implications on the sustainability since it
points out to the rising interest burden.

(iii) Capital outlay/ capital receipts

This ratio would indicate to what extent the capital receipts are applied for capital
formation. A ratio of less than one would not be sustainable in the long term
inasmuch as it indicates that a part of the capital receipt is being diverted to
unproductive revenue expenditure. On the contrary, a ratio of more than one
would indicate that capital investments are being made from revenue surplus as

Government.

(iv) Tax receipts vs. Gross State Domestic Product (GSDP)

The receipts consist of State taxes and State’s share of Central taxes. The latter
can also be viewed as Central taxes paid by people living in the State. Tax

20


Chapter-I_ An Overview of the Finances of the State Government

receipts suggest sustainability, But the ratio of Tax receipts to GSDP would have
implications for the flexibility as well. While a low ratio would imply that the
Government can tax more, and hence its flexibility, a high ratio may not only
point to the limits of this source of finance but also its inflexibility. Time series
analysis shows that in case of Manipur this ratio has been fluctuating between
0.13 and 0.15 during 1996-97 to 1999-2000 and has declined to 0.07 in 2000-01.
Similarly, the ratio of State tax receipts compared to GSDP was constant at 0.02
during 1996-97 to 1997-98 and declined to 0.01 during 1998-2000 and increased
to 0.02 in 2000-01. This ratio suggests that the State Government had the option
to raise more resources through taxation to generate more revenue for capital
formation.

(a) Return on Investment (ROD

The ROI is the ratio of the earnings to the capital employed. A high ROI suggests हैं
sustainability. The table presents the return on Government's investments in
statutory corporations, Government companies, joint stock companies and co-
operative institutions. It shows that the ROI in case of Government of Manipur,
except for the year 1998-99, was nil during the period of five years ending
2000-01.

(vi) Capital repayments vs. Capital borrowings

This ratio would indicate the extent to which the capital borrowings are available
for investment, after repayment of capital. The lower the ratio, the higher would
be the availability of capital for investment. In case of Manipur Government this
ratio ranged between 0.36 and 0.59 during the period from 1996-97 to 2000-01.
This indicated lesser amount of funds being available on investment.

(vii) Debt vs. Gross State Domestic Product(GSDP)

The GSDP is the total internal resource base of the State Government, which can
be used to service debt. An increasing ratio of Debt/GSDP would signify a
reduction in the Government's ability to meet its debt obligations and therefore
increasing risk for the lender. In the case of Government of Manipur, this ratio
showed an upward trend and increased from 0.44 in 1996-97 to 0.66 in 2000-01.
This shows that Governments ability to meet its debt obligations gradually
reduced during five years ending 2000-01.

(viii) Revenue deficit/ Fiscal deficit

The revenue deficit is the excess of revenue expenditure over revenue receipts and
represents the revenue expenditure financed by borrowings etc. Evidently, the
higher the revenue deficit, the more vulnerable is the State. Since fiscal deficit
represents the aggregate of all the borrowings the revenue deficit as a percentage
of fiscal deficit would indicate the extent to which the borrowings of the
Government are being used to finance non-productive revenue expenditure. Thus
the higher the ratio the worse of the state because that would indicate that the debt
burden is increasing without adding to the repayment capacity of the State. There
was Revenue surplus during the period from 1996-97 to 1998-99, but the State
had gone into revenue deficits during 1999-2000 and 2000-01. This indicated a
steep decline in the financial position of the State.

21



a या - ecient 7

Sede Report for the year encded 31 March 200 udit Report for the year ended 31 March 2001

(ix) Primary deficit vs, Fiscal deficit -%
Primary deficit is the fiscal deficit minus interest payments. This means that the
less the value of the ratio the less the availability of funds for capital investment.
In case of Government of Manipur, this ratio has been between 0.16 and 0.80
during 1996-97 to 2000-01. The ratio although increased significantly during
1999-2000 to 0.80 has declined to 0.22 in 2000-01 showing less availability
funds for capital investment.

(x) Guarantees vs, Revenue receipts

Outstanding guarantees, including the letters of comfort issued by the
Government, indicate the risk exposure of a State Government and should
therefore be compared with the ability of the Government to pay wiz., 15 revenue
receipts. Thus, the ratio of the total outstanding guarantees to total revenue
receipts of the Government would indicate the degree of vulnerability of the State
Government. In case of Manipur this ratio remained static since 1997-98
indicating no improvement in the position.

(xi) Assets vs. Liabilities

This ratio indicates the solvency of the Government. A ratio of more than | would
indicate that the State Government is solvent (assets are more than the liabilities)
while a ratio of less than 1 would be a contra indicator. In respect of Manipur
State the ratio was steadily declining from 2.38 in 1996-97 to 1.45 in 2000-
Olindicating that the liabilities are fast overtaking the assets.

(xii) Budget

There was no delay in submission of the budget and their approval by the State
Legislature. Chapter-II of this Report carries a detailed analysis of variations in
the budget estimates and the actual expenditure as also of the quality of budgetary
procedure and control over expenditure. It indicates defective budgeting and
inadequate control over expenditure, as evidenced by persistent resumption
(surrenders) of significant amounts every year vis-d-vis the final modified grant.
Significant variations (excess/saving) between the final modified grant and actual
expenditure were also persistent.

(xiii) Accounts

There was delay in the submission of accounts by the treasuries/ departments
during 2000-01. Out of 104 divisions of Public Works Department, Electricity
Department, Irrigation and Flood Control/Minor Irrigation Department and Public
Health Engineering Department, in case of 41 divisions there were delays in
submission of accounts ranging from | to 6 months. In case of Forest Department
out of 32 divisions there was delay up to 10 months resulting in ola from
the monthly cash accounts/delay in accountal and finalisation of their accounts

In case of treasuries/sub-treasuries out of 12, th

accounts up to 3 months in case of 2 treasuries, yan submission

Chapter-I An Overview of the Finances of the State Government

1.12 Conclusion

The year 2000-01 witnessed Revenue deficit for the second consecutive year
during the period of five years ending 2000-01. This was due to utilisation of
borrowed funds for revenue expenditure including large interest payment thus,
making the financial condition of the State Government unsustainable. The
borrowed funds were also inefficiently employed as would be seen from
insignificant return on investment.

APPROPRIATION AUDIT AND CONTROL OVER
EXPENDITURE
EE Re ee | Introduction

2.1.1 In accordance with the provisions of Article 204 of the Constitution of
India, soon after the grants under Article 203 are made by the State Legislature,
an Appropriation Bill is introduced to provide for appropriation out of the
Consolidated Fund of the State. The Appropriation Bill passed by the State
Legislature contains authority to appropriate certain sums from the Consolidated
Fund of the State for the specified services. Subsequently, supplementary or
additional grants can also be sanctioned by subsequent Appropriation Acts in
terms of Article 205 of the Constitution of India.

2.1.2. The Appropriation Act includes the expenditure which has been voted by
the Legislature on various grants in terms of Articles 204 and 205 of the
Constitution of India and also the expenditure which is required to be charged on
the Consolidated Fund of the State. The Appropriation Accounts are prepared
every year indicating the details of the amounts on various specified services

actually spent by Government vis-a-vis those authorised by the Appropriation
Act.

2.1.3. The objective of appropriation audit is to ascertain whether the
expenditure actually incurred under various grants is within the authorisation
given under the Appropriation Act and that the expenditure required to be charged
under the provisions of the Constitution is so charged. It also ascertains whether
the expenditure so incurred is in conformity with the law, relevant rules,
regulations and instructions.

2.2 Summary of Appropriation Accounts - 2000-2001

The summarised position of original and supplementary grants/ appropriations
and expenditure thereagainst is given below—

2.4.1 The overall saving of Rs.878.91 crore was the result of saving of
Rs.964.68 crore in 70 cases of grants and appropriations offset by excess of
Rs.85.77 crore in 9 cases of grants and appropriations.

2.4.2, Supplementary provision made during the year constituted 83.79 per cent
of the original provision as against 67 per cent in the previous year.

2.4.3 Supplementary provision of Rs.122.88 crore made in 33 cases during th
year proved unnecessary in view of aggregate saving of Rs.227.94 crore
detailed in Appendix II.

2.4.4 In 11 cases against additional requirement of Rs.827.46 crore
supplementary grants and appropriations of Rs.1356.30 crore were obtained
resulting in savings in each case exceeding Rs.10 lakh, aggregating Rs.528.84
crore. Details of these are given in Appendix III.

2.4.5 The excess of Rs.67.19 crore under 8 grants and Rs.18.58 crore under |
appropriation require regularisation under Article 205 of the Constitution. Details
of these are given in Appendix IV.

2.4.6 In 6 cases, supplementary provision of Rs.32.67 crore proved insufficient
by more than Rs.10 lakh each, leaving an aggregate uncovered excess expenditure
of Rs.71.60 crore as per details given in Appendix V.

2.4.7 In 39 cases, expenditure fell short by more than Rs.1 crore in each case
and also by more than 10 per cent of the total provision as indicated in Appendix
VI. In 3 of the above cases (SI.No. 29,30,38) the entire provision totalling
Rs.19.77 crore was not utilised.

2.4.8 In 7 cases there were persistent savings in excess of Rs.10 lakh in each
case and 20 per cent or more of the provision. Details are given in Appendix VII.

Excessive/unnecessary re-appropriation of funds

2.4.10 Re-appropriation is transfer of funds within a grant from one unit of
appropriation where savings are anticipated to another unit where additional funds
are needed. Cases where injudicious re-appropriation of funds proved excessive
or resulted in savings by over Rs.10 lakh are given:in Appendix LX.

Expenditure without provision

2.4.11 As envisaged in the Budget Manual, expenditure should not be incurred on
a scheme/service without provision of funds therefor. It was, however, noticed
that expenditure of Rs.35.69 crore was incurred in 18 grants/appropriations as
detailed in Appendix X without the provision having been made in the original
estimates/supplementary demands and no re-appropriation orders were issued.

Anticipated savings not surrendered

2.4 12 (a) According to rules framed by Government the spending
departments are required to surrender the grants/appropriations or portion thereof
to the Finance Department as and when the savings are anticipated. However, at
the close of the year 2000-01 there were 68 cases in which large savings had not
been surrendered by the departments. The amount involved was Rs.852.06 crore.
In 38 cases, the amount of available savings not surrendered amounted to more
than Rs.1 crore in each case. Details are given in Appendix XT.

(b) In 19 cases Rs.114.76 crore was surrendered on the last day of March
2001 indicating inadequate financial control over expenditure. Details are given in
Appendix XII.

Drawal of money ahead of requirement and retention in deposit account

2.5.1 Rule 290 of Central Treasury Rules prohibits drawal of money from the
treasury unless it is required for immediate disbursement. It is not permissible to
draw money from the Treasury in anticipation of demands or to prevent lapse of
budget grants. In the following cases amounts were drawn and retained in deposit
account to avoid lapse of grants. Scrutiny of records revealed that four
departments had drawn Rs.8.15 crore during 1989-90 to 1999-2000 and credited
to deposit account as detailed in Appendix XIV.

2.5.2 The purpose of drawal, its utilisation and balance are detailed below—
(a) Social Welfare Department

The State Government allocated (March 1999) funds of Rs.2.11 crore to the
Department for purchase of 310 tonnes of Amul Skimmed Milk Powder. The
Department drew (March 1999) the amount and kept it under 8449-Other
Deposits. Out of this, Rs.1.11 crore was withdrawn by the Department leaving the
balance of Rs.1.00 crore in the deposit account (March 2001).

Again, the State Government released (March. 2000) Rs.2.07 crore to the
Department for implementation of various programmes (purchase of Skimmed
Milk Powder: Rs.2.06 crore, observance of World Breast Feeding Week: Rs.0.01
crore and purchase of Anganwadi materials: Rs.0.30 lakh). The Department kept
(March 2000) the entire amount under 8449-Other Deposits and no expenditure
was incurred till March 2001.

Thus, out of the total amount of Rs.4.18 crore kept in the deposit account, only
Rs.1.11 crore was utilised leaving the balance of Rs.3.07 crore lying in the deposit
account and the programmes remained unimplemented.

(b) Revenue Department

Test check (March 2001) of records of the Deputy Commissioner (DC), Imphal
West (covering the period from February 1995 to February 2001) revealed that an
amount Rs.26.58 lakh sanctioned in February 1990 towards relief assistance for

the Government sanctions of Rs.12.50° lakh for implementation of various

unutilised as of February 2001 in the form of banker’s cheque (16 numbers).

The broad objective of the programme was to provide education comparable
in quality with formal education to all the children of 6-14 age group who
remained out of formal education. Although the programme envisages
specific funding pattern between Centre and State there was short release of
the Central and State matching share. Despite short-release of fund, there
were huge savings. The planning process for the programme was also
Seriously inadequate. Despite specific guidelines, there was low coverage of
hilly and tribal areas and irregular supply of teaching and learning
materials. In-service training was not imparted to NFE functionaries and the
prescribed course of NFE was not followed. There was no proper testing and
certification of NFE students and no record was maintained regarding drop-
out students and students entered into formal education.

Highlights

Against Rs.3.49 crore (Central share: Rs.3.05 crore; State share: Rs.0.44
crore) provided during 2000-01, expenditure of Rs.1.26 crore was incurred
by the Department resulting in savings of Rs.2.23 crore. Reasons for such
huge savings were not intimated by the Department.

(Paragraph 3.1.6)

There was short-release of State matching share of Rs.2.20 crore during
1995-96 to 2000-01.
(Paragraph 3.1.7)

Government of India had not released the second instalments of Rs.1.28
crore for the years 1998-99 and 1999-2000 due to the failure of the State
Government to fulfil the conditions.

(Paragraph 3.1.8)

NFE funds of Rs.48.32 lakh for training and learning material etc. were lying
in Deposit Account since March 1997, है

be established in audit,

Students learning requirements were not assessed and supplied from time to

was available.

(Paragraph 3.1.17)

The prescribed condensed courses of two years for primary and three years
for upper primary were not followed.
(Paragraph 3.1.18)

Against 100 centres required for each project, the shortfall of centres per
project during 2000-01 was 43 per cent. Against 25 students per centre, the
shortfall in enrolment was 16 per cent during 2000-01.

(Paragraph 3.1.20)

Strategy of testing and certification of NFE students for entering into formal
education was not done and no information of students entered into formal
education was available.

(Paragraphs 3.1.22 and 3.1.23)

Introduction

3.1.1 With a view to achieve the universalisation of Elementary Education, the
Non-Formal Education (NFE) programme was conceived in 1979-80 and was
scaled up in 1987 and revised in 1993. The aim of the programme was to provide
elementary education to the children in the 6-14 age group who remained out of
formal education and to bring them in the mainstream of formal education.
Overall responsibility of planning of the programme vested with the Government
of India while the responsibility of implementation vested with the State
Government and the Voluntary Agencies engaged in the field of education. The
scheme, however, ceased to exist after 31 March 2001.

Objective

3.1.2 The broad objective of the programme was to provide education
comparable in quality with formal education to all the children of 6-14 age group
who remained out-of formal education under State sector and to effectively
involve voluntary agencies in the implementation of the programme. The specific

Organisational set up

3.1.3 The implementation of the programme was transferred from the Director
of Education (Schools) in January 1990 to the Director of the State Council of
Educational Research and Training (SCERT). He was the nodal officer under the
overall charge of the Commissioner of Education Department. The
implementation of the programme was also entrusted to the voluntary agencies
with the cent per cent Central fund. The organisational set up of the Department is

given in the flow chart below—

Chapter II Civil Departments

Funding pattern

3.1.5 The programme was implemented through the State as well as Voluntary
Agencies (VAs). The Central assistance to the State was provided on sharing
basis between Central Government and State Government in the ratio of 60:40 for
co-educational centres and administrative resources support, and 90:10 for
exclusively girls’ centres, and cent per cent for Voluntary Agencies (VAs) on the
basis of number of NFE centres being run by them.

Financial management

3.1.6 Mention was made in paragraph 3.1.9.1 of the Report of the Comptroller
and Auditor General of India for the year ended 31 March 2000 regarding budget
provision made, funds sanctioned and expenditure incurred during 1995-96 to
1999-2000 under the programme. During 1995-96 to 1999-2000, the budget
provision and release of funds were Rs.15.31 crore (Central share: Rs.13.16crore;
State share: Rs.2.15 crore) and Rs.12.08 crore (Central share: Rs.10 crore; State
share: Rs.2.08 crore) respectively against which Rs.9.95 crore was expended
resulting in a savings of Rs.2.13 crore. During 2000-01 also there were
considerable savings of Rs.2.23 crore as against the budget provision and release
of funds of Rs.3.49 crore (Central share: Rs.3.05 crore; State share: Rs.0.44 crore)
the expenditure incurred was only Rs.1.26 crore. Reason for such huge savings
was not intimated by the Department.

Less provision of State’s share

3.1.7 The Central grants sanctioned, due for release along with State’s share due
and actually provided are detailed in Appendix XVI. Of the total funds of Rs.16.16
crore due for release by the Government of India to the State during 1995-96 to
2000-01, the State Government was required to provide funds of Rs.4.72 crore as
its share. Against this, Rs.2.52 crore was provided in the State budget and was
released. Thus, there was short provision of Rs.2.20 crore as State’s share.

Non-release of second instalments by the Central Government

3.1.8 Central funds were released in two instalments and the second and final
instalments were released after adjusting the unspent balance of previous year, on
utilisation of 75 per cent of the first instalment and furnish a progress report along
with the statement of expenditure as per terms and conditions of sanctions by the
State Government, Test-check of records revealed that second instalments during
1998-99 and 1999-2000 were not released by the Government of India due to
failure of the State Government to comply the conditions as detailed below—

Chapter III Civil Departments
Village Education Committee

3.1.13 The Village Education Committees were required to select suitable
location for centres, identify potential instructors, persuade parents to send their
children to the centres efc, and maintain liaison with other developmental
agencies at village level. However, no record was maintained and produced to
audit regarding establishment of Village Education Committees. Thus, the basis
of selection of NFE centres, identification of potential instructors and
identification of out-of formal education children was not on record.

In-service training

3.1.14 Mention was made in paragraph 3.1.9.3 of the Report of the Comptroller
and Auditor General of India for the year ended 31 March 2000 regarding non-
imparting of any training to the NFE functionaries, specially instructors and
supervisors despite sanction of funds of Rs.1.47 crore for training during 1995-96
to 1999-2000. Although Rs.31.20 lakh was also sanctioned during 2000-01 for
“this purpose, no training was imparted during the year. Reasons for this were not
intimated. Rupees 37.36 lakh sanctioned by the Government of India during
1996-97 continued to be retained under “Other Deposits" till date (June 2001).

Opening of NFE centres in area where formal education schools exists

3.1.15 The objective of the programme was to impart non-formal primary
education to children from habitation without school. Scrutiny of records of four
NFE projects test-checked, revealed that 70 NFE centres out of 241 were recorded
to have been opened and run by four NFE projects in the same village/area where
formal education schools were run by the State Government and District
Councils. These centres did not also exist (April 2001) due to discontinuance of
the programme after 31 March 2001.

Low coverage of hilly and tribal areas

3.1.16 The thrust of NFE programme was given to the children of 6-14 years age
group of hilly, tribal areas and urban slums. Records of the Directorate, test-
checked, showed that during 1995-96 to 2000-2001, 44 to 56 per cent of the total
learners only belonged to Scheduled Tribes and hilly areas.

Supply of teaching-learning material

3.1.17 Timely supply of books and teaching-learning material was the principal
strategy to achieve the objective of the programme. Test-check of the records of
the Director, however, revealed that no stock register showing receipt and
distribution of material could be shown to audit for examination. Scrutiny of the
stock registers of Project Officers test-checked disclosed that text books and
learning material had not been received in the projects and centres after March
1998.

Physical targets and achievement

3.1.19 Mention was made in paragraph 3.1.9.2 of the Report of the Comptroller
and Auditor General of India for the year ended 31 March 2000 regarding the
shortfall of NFE centres under NFE and enrolment during 1995-96 to 1999-2000.
The target and achievement during 2000-01 under the programme are given in
Appendix XVII.

3.1.20 Against 100 centres required for each project, the shortfall of centres per
Project was 43 per cent during 2000-01, and against 25 students per centre, the
shortfall in enrolment was 16 per cent during 2000-01.

Sub-optimal class strength

3.1.21 Guidelines provided that a NFE centre could be opened with 20-25
eligible children. It was, however, noticed that out of 63 centres under Kangpokpi
NFE project in Senapati District, 39 NFE centres were having 9 to 19 students
only.

after completion of the course. Of the 10,402 students enrolled in 400 centres,

Drop-out students and students entered into formal education

3.1.23 No information on drop-out students midway was available as the office
of the Project Officer (HQ), SCERT was gutted by fire twice (1994 and 1997)
during the period. No information on the students entered into the formal
education was also available in both State and VA sectors.

Flexibility and decentralisation of management

3.1.24 Guidelines emphasised on flexibility and decentralisation of management.
It was, however, noticed that the programme was being implemented centrally by
the Director through the Project Officers who had not been given financial power
to manage and maintain the centres.

Extra expenditure on account of entertainment in excess of sanctioned staff

3.1.25 Mention was made in paragraph 3.1.12.3 of the Report of the Comptroller
and Auditor General of India for the year ended 31 March 2000 regarding
expenditure of Rs.1.22 crore on the excess staff during 1998-99 and 1999-2000.
Scrutiny, further revealed that there were also excess staff during December 2000
to March 2001 (Accounts Clerk: 18, LDC: 16 and Peon: 7) on which Rs.5.82 lakh
was spent.

Monitoring

3.1.26 In order to ensure effective monitoring of the programme at Centre and
State level, the guidelines emphasised regular submission of quarterly progress
reports. But no information on the submission of the progress reports from the
Project to the State level and from the State to Central Government was available.
There was no district level authorities to provide technical and academic support
to the implementation of the programme. Thus, the link of monitoring,
supervision and control on the implementation of the programme did not exist.

Evaluation

3.1.27 The impact of the programme was not evaluated either at the State or
District level. The stress was mainly on expenditure rather than on achievement of
contemplated end-objectives of improving access to education for out-of school
children.

3.1.28 The above points have been referred to Government in September 2001;
reply has not been received (December 2001).

(Paragraph 3.2.31)

Introduction

3.2.1 _ In the context of the decision taken (20 March 1999) by the Committee of
National Development Council stressing joint effort by the Centre and the State
for bringing out medium term fiscal corrections in the finances of the States,
Government of India (GOI) instructed each State to identify a workable plan of
action. Accordingly, a Memorandum of Understanding (MOU) was signed (19
April 1999) between GOI, Ministry of Finance and the Government of Manipur
(GOM) regarding measures to be adopted by GOM to deal with the situation
arising out of drawal of overdraft of Rs.95 crore as on 17 April, 1999 by the
GOM. हि

Cash Deficit

3.2.5 Transfer of funds to Public Account towards the close of the — ला रा
was identified in the MOU as one of the problems of cash Lea Such
transfer of funds besides inflating the plan expenditure forms a committed
liability for the following years. In order to take appropriate measures to a
balance by removing these liabilities and ensure incurring of full plan expenditure
without going into frequent overdrafts, the GOM agreed to review all the Personal
Ledger Accounts and other Deposit Heads and initiate a proposal to write off the
balances more than 2 years old in consultation with the Accountant General (AG),
Manipur. The State Government also agreed (April 1999) to spread the plan
expenditure evenly with a maximum accumulation of up to 3 months meaning
thereby that in any month, plan expenditure would not exceed one fourth of the
total plan expenditure budget.

3.2.6 Test-check of records revealed that the Finance Department submitted
(June 2000) a proposal to the Accountant General (A&E), Manipur for writing off
of Rs.12.96 crore lying under Major Head 8449-Other Deposits which had
accumulated due to transfer of funds from annual budgets during 1986-87 to
1998-99 relating to 11 departments. The Accountant General pointed out (14
September 2000) that the Central Treasury Rules (CTRs) did not permit writing
off deposits and that the Government could consider treating such deposits as
lapsed and simultaneously crediting the same to Consolidated Fund under Rule
635 of CTRs.

3.2.7 The Finance Department stated (July 2001) that most of the amounts
pertained to Central funds. Since those could not be utilised for the purposes for
which those were sanctioned, those might have to be refunded to the Government
of India and this exercise would not help the State finances. No. concrete action
was therefore taken on the writing off balances under 8449-Other Deposits. At the
end of 2000-01, these balances stood at Rs.82.93.crore, Under 8443- Civil
Deposits, 106- Personal Deposits, which account for the balances of P i nal
Ledger Accounts a sum of Rs.3.50 crore was lying at the end of 2000. oT ;

Revision of Cinema Show Tax from Rs.5 to Rs.100

3.2.14 The Assam Amusement and Betting Tax (Manipur), 47 Amendment 1998
came into force with effect from 1 August 1998 revising cinema show tax from
Rs.5 to Rs.100. The Commissioner of Taxes stated (August 2001) that revised
rate could not be implemented in view of a case filed (1999) by Cine Exhibitors
Association in the Hon’ble Guwahati High Court, Imphal Bench which is still
pending in the court of law. No stay had, however, been granted by the High
Court. Revenue loss suffered by Government due to non-implementation of
revised rates of cinema show tax during 1999-2001 was Rs.41.61 lakh°.

Chapter II Civil Departments

3.2.17 Though the water tariff was increased by 100 per cent, revenue collection
did not increase proportionately in 2000-01 as compared to the previous year in
the’case of water charges’, Revenue collection however, decreased by 34 per cent
during 2000-01 in case of hospital charges®. Reason for the decrease was not
analysed by the department and hence the objective of mobilising additional
resource could not be fulfilled.

Public Demand Recovery Act

3.2.18 The Public Demand Recovery Bill 1999 cleared by the State Cabinet
(April 1999) with the objective of effecting recovery of public demands viz.
arrear of revenue, money payable to Government in repayment of loans, money
fixed as water rates, electricity tariff, motor vehicle taxes, sales tax efc. in the
State of Manipur was pending with GOI for assent (July 2001). After assent from
GOI, the State Government was to bring in an ordinance making it immediately
effective pending legislation. Pending clearance from GOI, the State Government
also committed to recover at least 20 per cent of the outstanding dues as on 31
March 1999 during 1999-2000.

3.2.19 It was however, noticed that pending clearance from GOI, State
Government could recover Rs.43.42 lakh only being the outstanding dues against
Rs.9.79 crore agreed upon in the MOU (i.e. 20 per cent of Rs.48.95 crore as on 31
March 1999). The actual recovery made during 2000-01 against the outstanding
dues of Rs.50.99 crore as on 31 March 2000, however, could not be ascertained in
audit.

3.2.20 As brought out in the foregoing paragraphs mere upward revision of rates
of taxes brought in only negligible or nil additions to the revenue. Fixing of soft
targets, non-rationalisation of procedures, and belated implementation of revised
rates indicated lack of due diligence on the part of the Government in raising
additional resources.

Compression of Non-Plan Revenue Expenditure

Ban on upgradation and creation of posts, abolition of posts lying vacant for
more than two years

3.2.21 The Government issued orders in May 1998, November 1999 and March
2001 imposing restrictions in the matter of creation and operation of posts. These
orders inter alia, envisaged ban on upgradation and creation of posts, abolition of
posts lying vacant for more than two years, complete ban on direct recruitment,
termination of all appointments on contract, adhoc, substitute, casual etc. and non-
conversion of work charged staff into regular establishment.

3.2.22 Notwithstanding these ban orders, 2581 persons were appointed by the
Heads of the Departments/Executive Officers during 19 April 1999 to 31 March

3.2.24 Government of Manipur also agreed to complete a review of identifying

by re-allocation of functions.

3.2.25 Finance Department stated (July 2001) that a committee in this respect
was formed (November 1999). The decision of the Government on the report
submitted by the committee was awaited (July 2001).

Power Sector

3.2.26 The State has its own Power Department which purchases power from
National Hydro-electric Power Corporation, North Eastern Electric Power
Corporation, Meghalaya State Electricity Board etc. and sells it to the consumers.
The average rate of power tariff is Rs.1.35 per unit as against the purchase rate of
Rs.2.35 per unit. The difference in the costs was allowed as subsidy to the
consumers. The last revision of power tariff took place in July 1998. To improve
the collection the Government committed to take the following measures—

(i) Realise at least 40 per cent of power dues during 1999-2000,

Transmission & Distribution /Commercial losses

3.2.29 As per information furnished by the Power Department the T&D/
Commercial losses during 1999-2000 and 2000-01 were 61 per cent and 58 per
cent respectively. Thus the department could bring down T&D/Commercial losses
by 3 per cent only during the year 2000-01 against the commitment of 10 per cent
by the State Government (shortfall being 7 per cent). As a result T&D losses
valuing Rs.4.38 crore’? could not be arrested by the Government.
T&D/Commercial losses during the year 1998-99 has not been intimated by the
department (December 2001).

Involvement of local bodies

3.2.30 Regarding involvement of local bodies in the collection of power dues the
Department had introduced a system in February 1999 under which collection of
arrears of electricity dues as well as current dues were entrusted to them on
payment of commission of 4 and 2 per cent respectively. This system was not
reviewed in August 1999 as agreed in the MOU. The Department stated (June
2000) that the local bodies had been involved in revenue collection only.

Disinvestment

3.2.32 The State Government has identified 10 Public Sector Undertakings
(PSUs) in the State which are incurring huge losses.

3.2.33 The State Planning Department submitted a report (June 2000) on the
issue. Based on the status of the PSUs including their financial health, the nature
of functions efc., out of 14 PSUs, 57 were categorised for consideration for
privatisation, 21 for winding up, 1'° for scaling down. Final decision of the State
Government was awaited (July 2001).

3.2.34 However, the State Government constituted (April 2001) a Fiscal Reforms
Commission to make recommendations on the functioning of the State PSUs and
suggestions for their revival/closure. The recommendations of the Committee

were awaited (July 2001).
Monitoring and Evaluation

3.2.35 As the implementation of the assurances in MOU involves more than one
department, proper monitoring should have been done by the nodal department.
The State Government had not evaluated the implementation of MOU.

3.2.36 The above points have been referred to Government in September 2001;
reply has not been received (December 2001).

Recommendations

3.2.37 Assurances given by the State Government as regards regular raising of
additional resources and applying of economy measures were not fulfilled. The
State Government should take adequate steps to fetch additional revenue, recover
outstanding dues, curtail non-plan expenditure on other items and bring down
losses of Power Department by raising collection-of revenue, The functioning of
the PSUs should be closely monitored by the State Government. 5

Chapter II] Civil Departments

[3.3 National Programme for Controlof Blindness

National Programme for Control of Blindness (NPCB) was launched in the
year 1976 with the aim of reducing blindness from 1.4 per cent to 0.3 per
cent by 2000 AD. A review of the working of the department/District
Blindness Control Societies (DBCSs) revealed that out of 4 DBCS centres
only one surgeon had been trained in Intra—Ocular Lense (IOL) Cataract
Surgery. In the absence of the trained Ophthalmic Surgeons in the State,
the main objective for reducing blindness by performing cataract surgeries
was frustrated.

Highlights

Against total grants of Rs.78.61 lakh received from the Government of India
during 1996-97 to 2000-01, Rs.9.19 lakh had not been released by the State
Government to the department for implementation of the programme as of
March 2001.

(Paragraphs 3.3.4 & 3.3.5)

Against total grants of Rs.43 lakh released by the Government of India
during 1996-97 to 2000-01, Rs.24.65 lakh was received by the DBCS. The
discrepancy of Rs.18.35 lakh was not reconciled with the Government of
India.

(Paragraphs 3.3.6 & 3.3.7)

The State Government appointed staff at different grades on contract basis
in the time scale of the State Government instead of consolidated pay as per
norms fixed by the Government of India for the period from December 1999
to January 2001 resulting in extra expenditure of Rs.4.36 lakh towards salary
constituting more than 10 per cent of the expenditure incurred on the
programme.

(Paragraph 3.3.8)

Against the target of 10,000 cataract surgeries to be conducted during 1996-
97 to 2000-01, only 3,244 surgeries were done which resulted in shortfall of
achievement by 67.56 per cent.

(Paragraph 3.3.13)

Introduction

3.3.1. National Programme for Control of Blindness (NPCB) was launched in
the year 1976 as a 100 per cent Centrally sponsored programme with the
objective of reducing the blindness from 1.4 per cent to 0.3 per cent by 2000 AD,
The scheme of setting up a District Blindness Control Society (DBCS) in each
district. of the country was launched in 1994-95 with the objective of
decentralising the implementation of the programme to utilise the funds released
to the DBCS in an effective and efficient manner. The DBCS was expected to
enhance the coverage and improve quality of eye care services in the district. This
programme was implemented in the State from May 1979 for providing
ophthalmic care and curative, preventive and rehabilitative service to the
community. The scheme of setting up the DBCS was launched in the State in

1994-95 with coverage of 4 districts'®.

3.3.5 Against Rs.78.61 lakh released by Government of India during 1996-97 to
2000-01, the State Government released Rs.69.42 lakh to the ‘department and
there was a short release of fund to the tune of Rs.9.19 lakh. Out of Rs.69.42 lakh
released by the Government, the department incurred expenditure of Rs.40.64
lakh during the period from 1996-97 to 2000-01. Thus, there was overall savings
of Rs.37.97 lakh to the Government. The savings were attributed to late receipt of
fund from the State Government. Reason for short release of fund by the State
Government was not on record.

Irregularities in utilisation of Central Grant

Excess expenditure on salary

3.3.8 As per financial ceiling towards salary, Rs.5.88 lakh Lage i. Le
respect of 11 officials of different categories for the period from eae ees
to January 2001 whereas Rs.10.24 lakh was spent during the sai = :
resulted in excess expenditure of Rs.4.36 lakh which constituted more than 10 per
cent of the expenditure on the programme.

Plan of action

3.3.9 To implement and monitor the activities during a particular year, the
DBCS was required to prepare a Plan of Action for that year and the same was to
be sent to the State Programme Officer. Although the Director of Health Services
(DHS) fixed the target of 2000 cataract operations each year with the instruction
to submit the Annual Action Plan, no plan of action for the years 1996-97 to
2000-01 was prepared and sent to the State Programme Officer.

3.3.10 As per the programme (NPCB), the number of blind people was to be
reduced from 1.4 per cent to 0.3 per cent by 2000 AD. But no assessment survey
was conducted by the DBCS to ascertain the number of blind people in the State.
As a result, percentage of blind people with reference to total population in the
State could not be ascertained.
