. Chapter III Civil Departments

3.3.13 Against the target of 10,000 cataract surgeries during the years 1996-97 to
2000-01, only 3,244 numbers of surgery were conducted resulting in shortfall of
achievement by 67.56 per cent. Audit noticed (June 2001) that the low
achievement was mainly due to shortage of trained surgeons in Intra-Ocular Lens
(OL) surgery and operating microscope as out of the 4 surgeons posted in the
DBCSs, only one surgeon was trained in IOL surgery.

(b) Camp organised

3.3.14 No target was fixed for number of camps to be organised by the Director
of Health Services for the years 1996-97 to 2000-01.

3.3.15 Test-check of records revealed that three DBCSs (Imphal, Bishnupur and
Churachandpur) organised 180 camps during 1996-97 to 2000-01 and 36,737
patients were examined and 967 cataract surgeries were performed. Out of 180
camps, 109 camps were organised by the Central Mobile Unit for 4 non-DBCSs
(Ukhrul, Tamenglong, Chandel and Senapati) and Imphal DBCS for which no
separate District Mobile Unit was created. The Central Mobile Unit organised
only 37 camps in the 4 non-DBCS districts and interior places of Imphal DBCS
during 1996-97 to 2000-01 and 298 cataract surgeries were performed against the
target of 1650. As a result, there was shortfall of achievement by 82 per cent.

Rehabilitation of the incurably blind

3.3.16 No programme for rehabilitation of the incurably blind was made in this
State. Reasons for non-implementation of the programme were not stated.

Information, Education and Communication (IEC)

activities under NPCB was neither prepared nor the production of local specific
print material films, software for electronics media was developed by the State
Programme Officer. NGOs were as not involved in these activities. Out of Rs.7

Rs.1.70 lakh was incurred on display hoardings ४८. leaving an unspent balance of

3.3.20 No expenditure was incurred by the DBCSs for renovation and furnishing

3.3.21 No expenditure was incurred for maintenance/servicing of ophthalmic

Eye Banks

3.3.22 Development of Eye Bank is an important activity for the problem of
corneal blindness. But no Eye Bank was established in the State to solve the
problem of corneal blindness.

Voluntary organisations

3.3.23 Till June 2001, no voluntary organisations were induced to implement the
scheme in tribal and rural backward areas where no eye care facility was
available.

Utilisation certificate

3.3.24 The utilisation certificate of the grant received under the programme of
NPCB implemented through DBCSs was to be sent to the Programme Division,
Ministry of Health and Family Welfare, Government of India within a period of
three months of the closing of a financial year.

3.3.25 Test-check of records revealed that utilisation certificates in respect of
DBCS, Imphal for the years 1997-98 to 1999-2000 were sent to the Government
of India in April 2001. As a result, Central assistance was not released to the

re eH! Civil Departments, III Civil Departments

SS
DBCS, Imphal for the years 1999-2000 and 2000-01. Similarly, grants were not
released to DBCS, Thoubal and Churachandpur for the years 1999-2000 and
2000-01 due to non-submission of utilisation certificates to the Government of
India in time.

Training

3.3.26 Test-check of records revealed that no training arrangements were made
for Medical Officers of PHCs, PMOAs, Nurses and Operation Theatre assistants
by any of the DBCSs. Reasons for not arranging any training were not stated by
the department. Due to shortage of trained doctors and nurses in the District
Hospitals, District Mobile Units and PHCs, the implementation of the programme
was frustrated apart from deprival of benefit to the targeted people for getting free
treatment as per programme.

Monitoring

3.3.27 No monitoring was conducted by the Commissioner of Health and the
Director of Health Services on the implementation of the programme in the State.
Moreover, no periodical meetings were held to review the implementation of the
programme at various levels and to suggest measures for its uniform and speedy
implementation which led to the major failure in the implementation of the
programme.

3.3.28 The above points have been referred to Government in September 2001;
reply has not been received (December 2001).

Recommendations

3.3.29 (i) Utilisation certificate with Annual Audited Statements of
expenditure need to be furnished to the Government of India within the stipulated
period (30 June) to get the Grant of Central assistance under NPCB in time for
speedy implementation of the programme.

(ii) Surgeons trained in Intra-Ocular Lens Surgery should be posted to the
District Mobile Ophthalmic units for cataract surgery either at camps or at
hospitals.

(iii) Field visits should be made regularly and the implementation of the
programme closely monitored. Records of cataract surgery and camps organised
are required to be maintained with annual target and achievement.

(iv) Training arrangements need to be made for Surgeons, Health Workers,
PMOASs, Medical Officers of PHCs, Nurses and Operation Theatre Assistants.

Audit Report for the year ende

The main objective of National Tuberculosis Control Programme is cure
of infectious cases through administration of directly observed short
course of Chemotherapy to all TB diagnosed patients to achieve a cure
rate of over 85 per cent. A review of the programme revealed that the
funds received from Central Government were not fully utilised due to
non-release of fund by the State Government. Against the prescribed
norms of 85 per cent, cure rate of new smear positive was 65.9 per cent.
The implementation of the programme was not monitored.

Highlights

Central assistance of Rs.8.05 lakh for 2000-01 remained unutilised due to
non-release of fund by the State Government.

(Paragraph 3.4.6)

Non-utilisation of Anti TB drug worth Rs.5.99 lakh purchased by the DHS,
Manipur during 1999-2000 had frustrated the objective of covering the
targeted patients.

(Paragraph 3.4.11)
Civil work for establishment of 15 Microscopy.centres in 4 districts had not
been taken up causing delay in implementation of the programme.

(Paragraph 3.4.13)

Detection of new cases decreased during 1997-98 to 2000-01. No survey to
identify new cases was conducted.

(Paragraph 3.4.20)

Cure rate of new smear positive cases during 1998-99 and 1999-2000 was
65.9 per cent as against the prescribed norms of 85 per cent.

(Paragraphs 3.4.22 & 3.4.23)

and $3 Multipurpose Health Workers (MPHWs) of 6 districts are yet to be

Introduction

3.4.1 The National Tuberculosis Control Programme (NTCP), a centrall
sponsored scheme, was launched by the GOI in 1963 to control गज का ड (TB)
in the country. Based on the findings of the committee of experts set up by the
Government of India in 1992, a revised strategy for National Tuberculosis Control
Programme (RNTCP) was introduced with emphasis on cure of infectious cases
7 ee आर



Chapter III Civil Departments

through administration of directly observed short course of chemotherapy to all
TB diagnosed patients to achieve a cure rate of over 85 per cent. The modified
programme (RNTCP) was implemented in the State from 1 September 1998 in 2
districts (Imphal East and Imphal West) only. Remaining 7 districts are under
Project Preparatory state. The main activities to be executed under the programme
were creation of TB unit (TU) at sub-district level for about 5 lakh population in
plain area and 2.50 lakh in hill area and diagnostic centre viz., Microscopy centre
covering maximum of 1 lakh population in plain area and 50000 in hill area in
PHC for sputum smear examination to test incidence of TB and treatment of cases
with anti TB drugs.

are indicated in the flow chart given below—

Irregularities in utilisation of Central assistance

3.4.11 Government of India released Rs.10.80 lakh during 1999-2000 to the State
Government as per requirement of fund as assessed by the DHS, Imphal for
procurement of anti TB drug for 1260 sputum negative cases for the year 1998-
99. Accordingly, DHS placed orders (September 1999) for medicine worth
Rs.10.76 lakh for covering 12547 patients. Medicines worth Rs.10.76 lakh were
received between September 1999 and March 2000. Of this, medicine worth
Rs.4.77 lakh only for 5567 patients were issued (January 2000 to June 2000) to
STO for subsequent distribution to DTOs. The balance medicine (Rs.5.99 lakh)
for 698 patients had not been utilised as of June 2001. The non-utilisation of
medicine not only resulted in locking up of Fund (Rs.5.99 lakh) but also frustrated

the objective of covering the targeted patients.

Treatment

3.4.21 The Government of India fixed the targets for identifying sputum positive
cases during 1998-99 and 1999-2000 at 1094 and 1110 (@ 50 cases per one lakh
population) on the projected population of 21.87 lakh and 22.14 lakh respectively.
Against this, achievements made during 1998-99 and 1999-2000 were 504
(RNTCP districts: 306 and NTCP districts:198) during 1998-99 and 765 (RNTCP
districts: 524 and NTCP districts: 241) respectively, the percentage being 46 and
68.98 respectively. The achievements of 7 NTCP districts were very low.

3.4.22 The most important factor for the success of the programme is the cure
rate over 85 per cent of new smear positive cases brought under treatment 12-15
month earlier under RNTCP through administration of Direct Observation

Treatment (DOT).

3.4.23 It was seen from records that out of 830 smear positive cases brought
under treatment during 1998-99 (306 cases) and 1999-2000 (524 cases) in the
RNTCP district of Imphal, only 547 cases (1998-99: 147 and 1999-2000: 400)
were cured up to March 2001. The cure rate was 65.9 per cent against the
prescribed norms of over 85 per cent. Reasons for shortfall in achievement of cure
rate were not on record.

3.4.24 During 1999-2000 and 2000-01, the DTC, Imphal identified 1368
(1551-183) sputum positive cases up to December 2000. Out of which 981 cases

Cases of death

3.4.25 Of the total number of 12299 (RNTCP district: 3838, NTCP district: 8461)
registered/brought under treatment during 1996-97 to 2000-01, 109 patients died
and 1617 were defaulters”’, Death of 109 (RNTCP: 89, NTCP: 20) patients was
stated by the department to be due to their development of HIV/AIDS.

Non-retrieval of defaulters to treatment

3.4.26 Though treatment card and identify card were prepared and TB register
maintained, no attempt to retrieve 1617 (RNTCP: 271, NTCP: 1346) defaulters
towards treatment was made, frustrating the aim of the programme to control
infection.

Non-availability of X-ray facilities

3.4.27 The X-ray machine with Odelca camera installed in Churachandpur
(1980) was out of order from July 1995. Although, DHS requested (October
1999) M/s Siemens Ltd., Calcutta to make arrangement for repair of the X-ray
machine, no follow up action was taken to set the machine right. Owing to non-
availability of X-ray facilities, the patients were deprived of the public health
services.

3.4.28 The X-ray machine installed in the DTC, Imphal also remained idle since
1 April 2000 due to non-availability of X-ray films. Though the matter was
projected in the quarterly reports for March 2000 and March 2001 submitted to
the Government of India, no separate communication was made to the
Government of India for supply of the films. Due to non-availability of X-ray
films, the patients were deprived of the X-ray facilities to be provided free of cost.

Training

3.4.29 The National TB Institute (NTI), Bangalore, TRC, Chennai and LRS
Institute of TB and Allied Diseases, Delhi are imparting training to medical and
para-medical staff both in NTCP and RNTCP. Training courses are also being
conducted at the State and District level to train the staff in RNTCP. Number of
training programme proposed, training programme actually held and number of

medical and para-medical staff trained during 1996-97 to 2000-01 are detailed in
Appendix XIX.

Monitoring and evaluation

3.4.34 At the State level the STO is required to monitor the activities in the State.
There was no regular flow of information from the various DTCs. As a result, the
monitoring of the programme at the State level was not done. No evaluation to
assess the impact of the programme was ever made at the State level.

3.4.35 The above points have been referred to Government in September 2001;
reply has not been received (December 2001).

Recommendations

3.4.36 (i) Project preparatory phase of RNTCP designated to provide all the
infrastructural facilities should be completed on priority basis so that the RNTCP
can be implemented in the remaining 7 districts without further delay.

(ii) Appointment of all the key staff, MO-TB in particular should be made at
the earliest to make the programme more effective. Training in RNTCP to all the
medical and para-medical staff should also be provided early.

(iii) Binocular microscopes should be supplied to all the microscopic centres
newly established for carrying out sputum smear examinations.

(iv) Direct Observation Treatment (DOT) should be introduced in all the
districts.

(v) | Reasons for shortfall in achievement of various components of the
programme should be analysed annually.

(vi) Effective steps may be taken to ensure regular flow of information from
the DTCs to facilitate monitoring of the scheme at the State level.

Introduction

3.5.1 A review of the National AIDS Control Programme (NACP) covering the
period from its inception (September 1992) to 1996-97 conducted in audit was
included in paragraph 3.8 of the Report of the Comptroller and Auditor General of
India for the year ended 31 March 1997.

3.5.2. The Programme continued up to March 1999 as NACP (I) with all the
components. The NACP (II) was launched from April 1999 covering all the nine
districts with the components of (1) Targeted Intervention, STD & Condom
Promotion, (2) Information, Education and Communication (IEC), Blood Safety
and Voluntary Testing and Counselling (VTC), (3) Low cost AIDS care, (4)
Institutional Strengthening and (5) Intersectoral Collaboration.

Organisational set up

3.5.3 The Organisational flow chart of State AIDS Cell up to March 1998 and
Manipur State AIDS Control Society (MACS) from April 1998 onwards are

indicated below:

Financial Management

3.5.5 The programme was fully financed by the Government है India out of the
World Bank assistance. The Government of India released the funds to the State
Government up to the financial year 1997-98 and the State Government released
the funds for the programme. From 1998-99 onwards, the funds were directly
released to the MACS by the Government of India on the basis of the approved
Annual Plan for implementation of the programme.

Fund received by the State Government and expenditure thereof

3.5.6 During 1997-98, the State Government released Rs.100.47 lakh against
Rs.2 crore received from the Government of India. The expenditure incurred was
Rs.42.17 lakh only. Out of the balance, Rs.58.30 lakh was drawn by the
department on AC Bills in March 1998 to avoid lapse of budget grant and kept the
amount in the Deposit head under Public Account. The unspent Central funds
received during the year thus amounted to Rs.58.30 lakh.

3.5.9 Against the total receipts of Rs.12.89 crore (Government of India: Rs.9.47
crore, unspent balance from the State Government: Rs.2.43 crore and the
Miscellaneous receipts: Rs.0.99 crore) during 1998-99 to 2000-01, the

Diversion of fund

3.5.10 As per approved Action Plan for 1999-2000, Rs.90.36 lakh was allotted by
the Government of India for Civil Works under three components. Out of this
Rs.1.74 lakh was incurred for Civil Works and the balance amount of Rs.88.62
lakh was diverted and spent on IEC and Awareness campaigns.

3.5.11 Against the approved allocation of Rs.58.50 lakh for IEC and Awareness
for 1999-2000, Rs.3.21 crore was incurred resulting in excess expenditure of
Rs.2.62 crore by diverting funds for other components and unspent balance
received from the State Government.

3.5.12 Similarly, during 2000-01, Rs.1.20 crore was spent by the MACS for IEC
and Awareness against the approved allocation of Rs.78.40 lakh by diverting fund
for other components. Excess expenditure of Rs.41.32 lakh was not regularised by
obtaining approval from the competent authority.

3.5.13 During 1998-99 to 2000-01 the MACS issued various reagents, kits and
medicines worth Rs.7.08 lakh to different hospitals. The hospitals however
diverted these for purposes not connected with AIDS Programmes. This diversion
had frustrated the very purpose for which the kits and medicines were purchased.

Irregular expenditure

3.5.14 Out of the available balance of previous years and savings under different
components of the programme an amount of Rs.60 lakh was sanctioned by the
Executive Committee and spent (October 2000 to March 2001) by the MACS for
construction of 100-bedded extension ward at JN Hospital contrary to the
guidelines of the scheme which totally barred civil construction from out of the
funds of the programme.

Working of STD clinics

3.5.16 Nine STD clinics were working with support and assistance from NACO
for eight districts” and RIMS.

3.5.17 Test-check of records revealed that out of 9 STD clinics, only three clinics
(at Old District Hospital, Imphal; District Hospital, Churachandpur ; and RIMS)
were functioning regularly with STD specialist. The remaining six clinics were
not functioning properly due to shortage of specialists and Medical Officers
resulting in non-providing of free treatment facilities to the patients and failure to
detect the cases of HIV/AIDS. Moreover, STD equipment of those six districts
were kept idle in the Store. The cost of equipment were not recorded in the Stock
Registers. During 1997-98 to 2000-01, not a single STD Report was furnished by
the Thoubal and Senapati District STD Clinics.

Preventive Intervention for the General Community
Information, Education and Communication (IEC) and Awareness campaigns

3.5.18 Mention was made in paragraph 3.8.6.3 of the Report of the Comptroller
and Auditor General of India for the year ended 31 March 1997 regarding non-
fixing of any target under this component although Rs.1.25 crore on IEC activities
were spent during 1993-94 to 1996-97. The AIDS Cell and the MACS spent
Rs.4.93 crore on IEC activities during 1997-98 to 2000-01, but no target was

fixed during 1997-98 to 2000-01.

3.5.19 Various awareness camps, mass rallies, competitions, awareness training
courses for community leaders, religious leaders efc. were organised in different
places in the State. Besides, leaflets, hoardings etc. in different local languages
were distributed to different youth clubs, women groups, NGOs etc. In addition,

media channels were also utilised.

3.5.20 In the absence of targets fixed, item-wise achievements of the programme
could not be analysed and commented upon.

Family Health Awareness Campaigns(F HAC)

3.5.21 To raise the level of awareness on RTP/STD and HIV/AIDS in rural
areas and other vulnerable groups of the population and to make the people aware
about the service available in the public health system for management of
RTI/STD intensive campaigns were to be mobilised to facilitate early detection
and prompt treatment of RTI/STD. Targets, achievements, actual expenditure,
number of PHCs/Blocks covered are shown in Appendix XX.

3.5.22 The actual attendance in the campaigns was 5,24,536 against the total
targeted population of 27,25,283 indicating shortfall of 81 per cent. Against the
admissible expenditure of Rs.0.39 crore, the MACS spent Rs.1.45 crore which
resulted in excess expenditure of Rs.1.06 crore (272 per cent).

Voluntary testing and counselling

3.5.23 According to the NACP (II), one voluntary testing centre was to be
established in each district for joint testing of couples, training grass root level
health care workers in HIV/AIDS counselling and providing counselling services
through all blood banks in India and through STI clinics. No voluntary testing
centre was established in any district. This frustrated one of the main objectives of

the programme.
Excess Man Power

3.5.24 Against the sanctioned 36 posts of different categories, 59 posts were
operated resulting in extra expenditure of Rs.21.22 lakh towards salaries for the
period from May 1999 to July 2001.

Non-Contribution by NGOs

3.5.27 As per guidelines of NACO, at least 10 per cent of the total project cost
was to be contributed by the NGOs but no contribution was made by the NGOs
since inception and full cost of the project was met out of Central assistance. This
resulted in excess expenditure of Rs.36.63 lakh during 1998-99 to 2000-2001.

Irregular Purchase

3.5.28 The Chairman of the MACS purchased three vehicles (Rs.7.55 lakh)
during 1999-2000 without specific permission of NACO as directed by the
Government of India.

Training

3.5.29 Training management is an important function of programme
management. During 1999-2001, all the trainings of the trainers at various level as
well as Induction Training were to be completed. In the pre-project phase, the
tasks to be completed were (a) review of training curricula/module/materials, their
modification and where necessary developing new curricula (b) completion of
training of State level trainers (c) preparation of guidelines for district action plan
(d) printing of training material/modules and (e) preparation of time activity
schedule. Details of funds provided, expenditure incurred and achievements made
against targets during 1997-98 to 2000-01 as per records maintained by the
MACS are given in Appendix XXI1.

3.5.30 Although largets set were quite adequate, there were overall shortfalls in
achievement in respect of training of doctors (58 per cent), nurses (71 per cent)
and paramedics (79 per cent) during 1997-98 to 2000-01.

Chapter III Civil Departments
Procurement of equipment and installation thereof

3.5.31 AIDS Control Societies were required to take steps to ensure the
immediate installation of equipment on arrival by having a prior physical
inspection of the sites. They were to ensure availability of (a) electric
connections/power supply (b) space/accommodation for installation of equipment
(c) central storage facility at the Society office and (d) trained staff to man the
equipment.

3.5.32. Test-check of records revealed that one Refrigerated Centrifuge machine
with Speedometer and Timer, Remi KB-70 (costing Rs.1.25 lakh) was received
by RIMS in March 1994 from NACO for using in the blood component
preparation and the same was not used for the purpose (June 2001). In reply to
audit query it was stated that some more equipment were required for using the
machine. RIMS, however, did not take up the matter with the NACO. As such,
the machine remained idle (June 2001). Similarly, one FACS Counter (Rs.23.74
lakh) was received (February 2000) by RIMS from World Health Organisation
through NACO and installed (January 2001) after lapse of 11 months without
putting into operation (June 2001). It was stated by RIMS that the machine was
supplied by NACO without giving sufficient period of intimation and thus
delayed the installation. Reasons for non-operation of the machine was due to
non-receipt of some reagents from NACO.

Monitoring and Evaluation

3.5.33 A specific independent review on drug and condom quality, surveillance
and management was envisaged by the NACO to be conducted by an outside
agency in the second year of the project (2000-01). But the review was neither
conducted nor any evaluation of the programme was made (July 2001).

Reporting system

3.5.34 Progress Reports of implementation were to be sent to NACO by MACS
monthly, quarterly and annually. It was seen in audit that no quarterly report in
prescribed forms of OMR, PMR and PWR were sent to NACO.

3.5.35 The above points have been referred to Government in September 2001;
reply has not been received (December 2001).

Recommendations

3.5.36(i) Diversion from one sub-component/ component to other sub-
components/ components may be avoided.

(ii) | Advances made for various activities of the programme should be booked
as advance instead of booking under the final head of account.

(iii) It should be ensured that 10 per cent of the project cost is borne by the
NGOs and 90 per cent by the MACS.

[36 National Leprosy Control Programme ही 3.6 National Leprosy Control Programme ie
The objective of National Leprosy Control Programme was to eliminate
leprosy by the end of 2000 by reducing the case load to less than | per
10,000 population. A review of the programme revealed that the funds
received from Government of India as Central assistance were not fully
utilised affecting the implementation of the programme adversely. Against

the target, no voluntary bed was created and there were cases of
deformity due to failure in early detection and prompt treatment.

due to ban imposed by RBI (Rs.5.57 lakh) and non-release of fund by the
State Government (Rs.1.00 lakh),

to 2000-2001, no voluntary bed was created or maintained.

The aim of the programme to eliminate leprosy by the end of 2000 by
reducing the case load to less than 1 per 10000 population could not be
achieved in five districts*’ even at the end of March 2001.

(Paragraph 3.6.30)

Introduction

3.6.1 The National Leprosy Control Programme (redesignated as National
Leprosy Eradication Programme (NLEP) in 1983) was launched in Manipur in
1955 as a 100 per cent Centrally Sponsored Scheme. The objective of the

programme was elimination of Leprosy by the end of 2000 AD by reducing the
case load to less than 1 per 10,000 population. हैं

Setting up of Survey, Education and Treatment Centre (SET)

3.6.10 Under the Programme, the SET Centre is to be set up in endemic pockets
of low endemic district for coverage of a population of 25,000. The centre is to
arrange house to house survey, impart health education, detect cases and arrange
for treatment. In Tamenglong (population 86278 as per 1991 census) and
Bishnupur (Population 1,80,773) where prevalence rates ranged between 5.69 and
5.09 per ten thousand at the beginning of 1996-97, no SET was established as no
sanction was accorded by GOI for the purpose.

Maintenance of Voluntary Leprosy Beds

3.6.11 GOI fixed the target for maintenance of 20 Voluntary beds each year
during 1996-97 to 2000-01. But no bed was created so far (June 2001).

Reconstructive Surgery Unit (RSU)

3.6.12 Under the programme, the RSU is to be established with a view to provide
major or minor Surgical facilities to the patients cured but deformed. In the state,
the RSU was not set up as of June 2001 due to non-finalisation of accommodation
as stated by SLO (May 2001).

Creation of Mini Sentinel Centre (MSC)

3.6.13 Creation of one Mini Sentinel Centre was targeted during 2000-2001 with
the objective of assessing availability of Multi Drug Treatment (MDT) Services,
the patients care services and Monitoring Leprosy elimination indicators. The
centre has not been created so far (June 2001) as, selection of one doctor to be
appointed as Epidemiologist has not been made by the state Government till June
2001.

Upgradation of State Leprosy Cell (SLC)

3.6.14 The existing State Leprosy Cell is required to be upgraded as instructed by
the GOI (December 1999) by appointing one Budget and Finance Officer (BFO),
one Data Entry Operator (DEO) on Contract basis and one daily wage driver. No
action has been taken by the Health & Medical Department so far (June 2001) to
appoint the above staff for upgradation of the Cell.

3.6.15 Although 8 District Leprosy Units (one each in 8 districts) established
prior to 1996-97 are functioning, no Leprosy Rehabilitation-cum-Promotion Unit
and Regional Leprosy Training and Referral Institution were established in the
State so far (June 2001).

Multi-Drug Therapy (MDT)

3.6.16 One of the main objective of MDT is to prevent deformity by early
detection and prompt treatment. The general system of case detection followed by
the department was household survey, focal survey, rapid survey, contact survey,
school survey, skin test camp, voluntary reporting and referral services from
peripheral health institutions.

75

3.6.17 During 1996-97 to 2000-01, 160 cases of deformity were reported (May
2001) which indicated failure of early detection and prompt treatment of patients
The position of cases of deformity during 1996-97 to 2000-01 is shown in the

Table No. 3.18 below para 3.6.23.

Rehabilitation programme

3.6.18 Although medical rehabilitation like supply of, micro-cellular rubber
chappal, foot strap, below knee prosthesis, amputation of limbs and physiotherapy
services were provided to the patients, no programme for socio-economic
rehabilitation of the deformed patients was taken up (June 2001).

3.6.24 It was noticed from the record that seven relapsed cases were discharged
after treatment. Surveillance (bacteriological) as required after completion of
previous treatment was not conducted in 4° districts due to non-availability of

laboratory technicians.

Migrated/death cases

3.6.25 In the State, 14 cases of death, 17 migrated cases and one untraceable case
during 1996-97 to 2000-01 were on record. Reason for death as stated (June 2001)
by the department was old age. As regards migrated and untraceable cases, no
follow-up action was taken due to insufficient address of the patients as recorded
in the treatment card, registers etc.

Modified Leprosy Elimination Campaign (MLEO)

3.6.26 Under NLEP, the first round of MLEC was launched in March 1999 and
the second round during 2000-2001 (January 2000 to February 2001) with the

objectives to—

(a) Create mass awareness about facts of leprosy and carry out intensive
awareness campaigns.

(b) Give orientation training on leprosy to all the Health Care Staff, Village
level workers, volunteers and Medical Officers and Laboratory Technicians.

(c) Detect hidden or suspected cases within a short period of 6 to 7 days and
to give them MDT.

3.6.27 For detection of hidden leprosy cases, rapid survey to cover the whole
population of the state was conducted under MLEC-I in March 1999 in each
district by search teams. Out of 22,06,411 projected population in the state only
17,64,899 population was covered leaving 4,41,512 population uncovered by the

survey.

* Bishnupur, Ukhrul, Chandel and Senapati, 0

77


uci Report for 0222 2 I SG Report for the year ended 31 March 2001

3.6.28 During the survey, 4805 suspected cases were detected by the survey
teams. Of these, 222 cases were confirmed to be leprosy cases. The variation vs
due to the fact that the cases detected were due to survey done by teams
consisting of non-medical staff. These cases were subsequently examined by the
medical officers and actual leprosy cases confirmed by them.

Support from World Health Organisation (WHO)

3.6.29 Idle equipment: WHO also provided anti-leprosy drugs/equipments free
of cost from 1996-97 onwards for implementation of the programme. Test check
of records of the SLO revealed that 2 microscopes (out of 4) and 1 electronic
typewriter received (February 1994 and June 1992) respectively were lying idle
(June 2001).

Monitoring and evaluation

3.6.30 Monitoring of the Programme was done by SLO. The programme was last
evaluated in March 1999. Aim of the programme to eliminate leprosy by the end
of 2000 AD by reducing the case load to less than | per 10,000 population could
not be achieved fully even at the end of March 2001 in five districts as shown
below—

Table No. 3.19

SECTION “B” PARAGRAPHS

3.7.1 With a view to producing pulses and oilseeds, the Regional Pulses and
Oilseed Production (RP&OP) Farm at Gamphazol was established during the
Seventh Plan period with the financial assistance of the North Eastern Council
(Estimated cost: Rs.53.83 lakh). The farm was established in June 1986 and
started production since 1990-91.

3.7.2 Of 50 hectares of land under the farm, 30 hectares were cultivable. The
farm had 14 regular staff on its roll. Besides, there were one tractor, one power
tiller and one jeep since 1988.

3.7.3 Test-check (February 2001) of records of the Seed Production Officer,
RP&OP revealed that out of 30 hectares cultivable land, targeted area for
production was between 10 to 25 hectares of land only during the period from
1996-97 to 1999-2000. Shortfalls in fixing the target ranged from 17 to 67 per
cent. Reason for not targeting entire cultivable land for production during the said
period was neither on record nor stated. Even against this soft target, achievement
in cultivation of land fell short by 47 to 80 per cent during the years. The total
production of pulses and oilseeds was only 1,457 kg (5 per cent) against the target
of 27,000 kg during 1996-97 to 1999-2000 (as detailed in Appendix XXII). There
was no target and production during 2000-2001. Thus, in spite of having the
infrastructure, the production was negligible and no remedial measures were
taken. Expenditure of Rs.57.68 lakh (Salaries: Rs.49.65 lakh; Wages: Rs.4.50
lakh and Other Expenses: Rs.3.47 lakh) during 1996-97 to 2000-2001 (up to
January 2001) on the farm was, therefore, largely unproductive.

3.7.4. The Department attributed the poor production to damage by buffaloes
and non-availability of water during 1996-97, 1998-2000 and discontinuation of
cultivation due to utilisation of the machinery by the office of the State
Mechanised Farm (SMF), Lamphelpat during 1997-98. These reasons are not
tenable as protection of farm from stray cattle and availability of water would
have been taken into account while setting up the farm in 1986.

3.7.5 The matter was reported to the department/Government (June 2001);
replies had not been received (December 2001).

3.8.1 In order to contain the pests population below threshold levels through
release of laboratory reared bio-control agents, Government of India (GON),
Ministry of Agriculture, released (November 1994 to March 1996) Rs.50.00 lakh
as cent per cent grants-in-aid (Building : Rs.30.00 lakh, laboratory equipment:
Rs.17.50 lakh and vehicle : Rs.2.50 lakh) for setting up of a Bio-control
laboratory during VIII Plan period (1992-97) at Imphal under the Scheme
“Integrated Pest Management Centres”.

3.8.2 Test-check (February 2001) of records of the Assistant Engineer, Minor
Inigation-II, Imphal and Agriculture Officer, Plant Protection, Imphal for the
period from October 1995 to January 2001 revealed that construction of the
building was completed in August 1997 at a cost of Rs.30 lakh without providing
Sanitation, water supply and electrification works. Laboratory equipment valued
at Rs.17.50 lakh were procured in May 1997 and the balance amount Rs.2.50 lakh
was spent on vehicle. But the laboratory could not be set up for want of
completion of the sanitation, water supply and electrification works. Scrutiny,
however, revealed that for sanitation and electrification works, estimates (Rs.4.22
lakh) were framed only in April 2000 and were awaiting approval of the GOI
(May 2001).

3.8.3 Thus, due to improper planning, the Bio-contro] laboratory could not be
made functional even after 4 years of release of funds by the Centre and incurring
expenditure of Rs.43.28 lakh thereby frustrating the very purpose for which it was
sanctioned.

3.8.4 The matter was referred to Government (August 2001); reply had not been
received (December 2001).

3.9.1 Government of India (GOI), Ministry of Finance, Department of
Expenditure sanctioned (December 1999) Rs.1.25 crore as additional Central
assistance under Basic Minimum Service (BMS), 1999-2000 from the Non-
Lapsable Central Pools of Resources (NLCPR) for construction of additional
classrooms (84) for Elementary Schools (42) and Rs.6.17 crore in March 2000 as

80



iii ii mag eta ig 2-4: 14-4:4- 4491 Chapter III Civil Departments

Central assistance from NLCPR for construction of primary school buildings for
buildingless Primary Schools (Rs.0.80 crore; 205 primary schools) and extension
of 2 classrooms for State Government Secondary (172) and Higher Secondary
Schools (43) (Rs.5.37 crore). The sanctions were revalidated by GOI in June
2000. Both the amounts were to be utilised by 30 September 2000.

3.9.2. Scrutiny (September-November 2000) of records of the Director of
Education (School), Imphal, revealed that the entire amount of Rs.7.42 crore
(Rs.1.25 crore plus Rs.6.17 crore) was drawn (September 2000) by him in 3
Abstract Contingent (AC) bills, only to be deposited (September 2000) under
“8449—Other Deposits”.

3.9.3. Although the amount of Rs.7.42 crore was lying under “8449—Other
Deposits” as of November 2000, it was noticed that the Director of Education (S)
submitted (October 2000) utilisation certificates to the State Planning Department
stating that the amounts were fully utilised for the purposes for which these were
sanctioned.

3.9.4 The Director in his reply (May 2001) stated that out of the amount of
Rs.6.17 crore, Rs.2 crore was encashed (January 2001), with the permission
(December 2000) of the Finance Department, of which Rs.1.57 crore was
disbursed to contractors/beneficiaries and revised utilisation certificate for Rs.1.57
crore only (out of Rs.7.42 crore submitted earlier) was submitted (March 2001) by
him to the State Planning Department.

3.9.5 The Director further stated that the funds could not be utilised by 30
September 2000 as the amount had to be deposited under “8449—Other Deposits”
as per instruction of the State Finance Department. Thus, the department had
retained the amount unauthorisedly though the amount was to be refunded as per
order of Government of India. It also deprived the schools as well as the students
of the intended benefits.

3.9.6 The matter was reported to Government (August 2001); reply had not
been received (December 2001).

Non-deduction of sales tax of Rs.27.91 lakh from the supplier of photo
identity cards in violation of Government order. rendered undue financial aid
to the supplier

3.10.1 As per Notification (December 1990) of the Government of Manipur, any
person responsible for paying any sum on behalf of any department of the
Government etc. shall deduct sales tax at source while making payments to

81



el RO Oy Report for the year ended 31 March 2001

suppliers/contractors for works done or supplies made by them and the amount of
tax so deducted is to be deposited on behalf of the dealer by the office concerna
into the Government account within seven days from the date of deduction

3.10.2 Test-check (December 1999) of records of the Chief Electoral Officer,
Manipur revealed that it failed to deduct sales tax of Rs.27.91 lakh (at the
prescribed rate of 7 per cent) from the payment of Rs.3.99 crore made (during
March 1994 to June 1999) to MANITRON (a Government of Manipur
undertaking) against their claim of Rs.4.19 crore for supplying 10,33,733 number
of photo identity cards (PICs) for clectors. This indicated not only violation of
Government orders but also rendering undue financial aid to the supplier.

3.10.3 In reply (October 2001) the Joint Chief Electoral Officer (JCEO) stated
that the Department purchased inputs for preparation of photo identity cards
(PICs) for which sales tax was deducted from the supplier’s bill and supplied
those materials to MANITRON as part of the inputs for preparation of PICs. No
sales tax was therefore deductible from the payment made to MANITRON. The
contention of the Department is not acceptable as the value of inputs
purchased/supplied by the department to the firm is not included in total payment
of Rs.3.99 crore made to the firm which is the total value of the service contract
for preparation of PIC and sales tax was rightly deductible from the above
payments. This position has been clarified (December 2000) by the Commissioner
of Taxes that as per instructions of the Finance Department sales tax should have
been deducted at source before making payment to the firm.

3.10.4 The matter was reported to Government (September 2001); reply had not
been received (December 2001).

4

[eee = sanctions obtained by inflating the number of jail inmates |
resulted in irregular and excess expenditure of Rs.22.36 lakh

3.13.1 Expenditure sanctions towards diet money for prisoners/detenues in
different jails of Manipur are accorded by the Government at the prescribed rates
based on the number of jail inmates reported by the jail authority in their monthly

proposals.

3.13.2 In course of test-check (March 2001) of records of the Central Jail, Sajiwa
it was noticed that in the proposals for diet money, number of jail inmates
enrolled was inflated by the jail authority. During September 1998 to November
2000 the jail authority sent monthly proposals for sanction of diet money
amounting Rs.83.27 lakh for 557 (average) number of under-trial prisoners
(UTPs)/convicts (excluding cases where special hospital diet was prescribed) and
36 (average) detenues under National Security Act (DUNSA) against the enrolled
(average) number of 343 and 22 respectively during the period. The Government,
however, sanctioned diet money of Rs.79.80 lakh for the purpose during
September 1998 to November 2000 against which expenditure of Rs.73.60 lakh
for 365 inmates (average) was incurred by the jail authority against admissible
amount of Rs.51.24 lakh for 365 average enrolled numbers of UTPs/convicts.

3.13.3 Inflation of the total number of UTPs/convicts and DUNSA by 214 and 14
respectively for obtaining expenditure sanction was irregular and led to an excess
expenditure of Rs.22.36 lakh as shown in Appendix XXIII

3.13.4 On being reported by Audit, the Additional Superintendent of the jail
stated (April 2001) that the prescribed rates of the diet money, required to be
reviewed every year, were not enhanced after January 1997 till December 2000.
The reply was silent on why the number of jailmates was inflated.

3.13.5 The jail authority/department submitted proposals for diet money on
inflated figures of jail inmates for sanction instead of moving the Government for
revision of rates. This led to an irregular and excess expenditure of Rs.22.36 lakh
over the prescribed rates for diet money for jail inmates.

3.13.6 The matter was reported to department/Government (June 2001); reply
had not been received (December 2001).

3.14 Irregular drawal of arrear pay and allowances

{SGU saan nn ot pa violation of Government orders part of sa hte pay and allowances of
Rs.7.05 lakh was drawn in cash instead of depositing the same to the GPF
accounts

3.14.1 As per Government of Manipur, Finance Department (Pay Implementation
Cell) Office Memorandum of February 1999, arrears of pay and allowances (for
the period from 1-1-1996 to 31-12-1998) on fixation of pay of the Government
servants in the revised scales of pay under the Manipur Services (Revised Pay)
Rules, 1999 were to be deposited in their General Provident Fund (GPF) accounts
for a period of three years from 1-1-1999.

3.14.2 Test-check (December 2000) of records of the Chief Medical Officer,
Senapati revealed that out of the arrear pay and allowances of Rs.1 1.41 lakh in
respect of 37 employees, only Rs.4.36 lakh was deposited in their GPF accounts
and the balance amount of Rs.7.05 lakh was drawn in cash (July 1999) and
disbursed to the employees.

3.14.3 Thus, instead of depositing the whole amount of arrear pay and allowances
to GPF accounts the drawal and disbursement of Rs.7.05 lakh violating the
Government orders was irregular. It also entailed additional interest liability of
Rs.0.22 lakh” for the Government as the Government was borrowing market
loans at 12.25 per cent during the year 1999-2000 against 11 per cent payable on
Provident Fund balances.

3.14.4 The matter was reported to Government (September 2001) ; reply had not
been received (December 2001).

Introduction

4.1.1 The Accelerated Rural Water Supply Programme (ARWSP) was
introduced in 1972-73 to assist the States and the Union Territories with 100 per
cent grants in aid to implement the schemes in problem villages. But with the
introduction of the Minimum Needs Programme (MNP) during the Fifth Five-
year Plan from 1974-75, it was withdrawn. The programme was, however, re-
introduced in 1977-78 and continued till 1998-99. From 1.4.99, the scheme has
been revamped. During the Drought of 1987, Government of India decided to
extend the ARWSP to towns with a population of less than 20,000. Accordingly,
the Centrally Sponsored Accelerated Urban Water Supply Programme (AUWSP)
was launched in February 1994. The ARWSP and the AUWSP were implemented
in the State in 1972-73 and 1995-96 respectively.

Objective

4.1.2. The main objectives of ARWSP was to (i) ensure coverage of all rural
habitations especially to reach the partially covered/uncovered habitation with
access to safe drinking water (ii) ensure sustainability of the system and sources
(iii) preserve quality of water by institutionalising water quality monitoring and
surveillance through a catchment area approach. The objectives of the AUWSP
was to (i) improve the environment and quality of life and (ii) better socio-
economic conditions and enhance more productivity to sustain the economy of the
country.

Planning
4.1.12 During 1994, under the sponsorship of Central Government, All India

Institute of Hygiene and Public Health conducted a partial survey of the problem
habitations in the state. The survey revealed 473 number of problem habitations.
During 1996-97, the State Government conducted another partial survey
according to which there were 291 problem habitations. The department
maintained that these surveys could not reveal the entire picture of the state. But
for no reasons recorded, the department did not carry out a complete survey at any
point of time so as to detect actual position.

Chapter IV Works Expenditure

4.1.13 The department stated (August 2001) that water sources were identified by
conducting physical survey and using topographical maps but no satellite images
and hydrological maps were used. Except for testing the quality of water of the
sources no other considerations such as sustainability of the sources were taken
into account by the department. The fact was also admitted by the department. As
a result, 22 cases of source depletion during 1997-98, 4 during 1998-99 and 3
during 1999-2000 were noticed from the record.

4.1.14 The depletions were mainly due to vast deforestation as stated by the
department. No step was, however, taken up to check deforestations in
consultation with the Forest department.

4.1.15 Non-Government organisations or any other agencies were not involved in
implementing the programme as required under guidelines of Government of
India.

4.1.16 No sector reform and research and development projects were taken up by
the department as provided in the guidelines of the programme. The department,
stated (August 2001) that a project report submitted to Government of India
proposing Imphal West District as a pilot project for implementation of the sector
reform project has not been approved by GOI so far (November 2001).

4.1.17 In June 1996, a State Level Rajiv Gandhi Drinking Water Mission
Authority was formed for reviewing the progress of implementation, sanctioning
of posts of the Mission at appropriate levels and giving directions for
implementing the programme. This was followed by other committees such as,
the State Level Empowerment Committee, District Level Committees and
District-wise Task Force. Apex Committee, Executive Committee and District
Level Committees under the State Level Water and Sanitation Mission were
belatedly formed in December 1999. But, in the absence of any record,
functioning of the committees could not be assessed.

4.1.25 Between 1997 and 1999, seven schemes in three hill districts’ had been
abandoned midway after incurring expenditure of Rs.8.62 lakh due to
shifting/desertion of villages during 1997 to 1999.

4.1.26 The department had not taken up any programme of extension of drinking
water facilities to schools and Anganwadis as required under guidelines of GOI.
According to surveys conducted during 1997-98, three districts had 839 schools
and Anganwadis (Churachandpur: 91, Ukhrul: 273, and Thoubal: 475). After the
surveys, the divisions submitted proposals during 1997-98 to the respective
Deputy Commissioners of the districts for extension of drinking water facilities to
these schools. But the approval of the proposals was awaited (June 2001).

4.1.27 As per ninth plan proposal (1997-2000) the department contemplated to
remove quality problems from 550 tube wells, development of 500 numbers of
sources and upgradation of supply level from 40 to 50 LPCD in 1000 schemes.
However, no formal proposals were made by the Department. In absence of this
these works were not taken up till March, 2001. Reasons for not submitting
formal proposals were neither on record nor stated (November 2001).

4.1.28 During the four years, the State Government provided funds (Rs.1.72
lakh) for installation of hand-pumps for SC/ST (Rs.1.11 lakh) and for survey of
status of drinking water in rural areas (Rs.0.61 lakh). But no action was taken and
the entire fund remained unutilised as of March 2001.

Establishment of water testing laboratories

4.1.29 An effective quality monitoring and surveillance would eae least
istrict out of nine districts in the State. In addition to

e labora each 0 :
oe f India sanctioned (February

existing three testing laboratories Government 0 4 10166 (६
1998) Rs.12 lakh for setting up of another six laboratories वा six districts. The
Central Government advised (May 1998) the State to set up the laboratories
without any loss of time and to involve the State Health Department in the quality
surveillance and to establish institutional linkage with them. But despite provision
of Rs.24.01 lakh during 1998-99 to 2000-01 in the budget and existence of a full
fledged Monitoring Division in the Department, no expenditure was incurred by
the Department for establishing additional laboratories. Reasons were neither on
record nor stated.

4.1.30 The Mobile Laboratory (July 1991) carried out quality testing especially
of underground water from tube wells. The testing, however, limited to physio-
chemical tests only. The performance of the laboratory was extremely poor. From
January 1997 to April 2001 it tested only 132 samples - hardly an average of 3
samples per month.

4.1.31 The Stationary Laboratory at Churachandpur tested only 12 samples from
April 1997 to June 1997. Thereafter the lab was destroyed during an ethnic clash.
The department had not taken any step to set up a new laboratory.

Control of excess iron

4.1.32 Cases of fluorosis, arsenic and brackishness were not reported by the
department. The department however, stated that no such cases was detected in
any village. However, 181 cases of excess iron content were stated to have been
encountered in the underground water (i.e. tube well water). Up to the end of
March 1997 altogether 175 iron removal plants had been installed at a cost of
Rs.7.74 lakh, During 1997-2001, another 6 such plants had been installed in two
districts (Imphal East and Imphal West) at a cost of Rs.2.54 lakh. The department
could not furnish information on non-operational plants. Therefore, analysis of
reasons of their being non-functional and their impact on the mission could not be
commented upon. पु

Operation and maintenance of schemes

4.1.40 In May 1998, the Ministry advised the State Govt. to transfer Operation
and maintenance of rural water supply schemes to the local community to
inculcate a sense of ownership to them. Despite issue of notification (September
1998) by the Government in that regard the department did not hand over any of
the schemes implemented in the 2460 habitants to the users communities for their
operation and maintenance.

4.1.41 For the operation and maintenance of schemes covering 2398 habitations
(April 1997) to 2763 (April 2000), the State Government provided Rs.16.13 crore
during 1997-98 to 2000-01. But the department could spend only Rs.15.31.crore
leading to a saving of Rs.0.82 crore during the four years.

4.1.42 As per information furnished by the department’ 220 schemes were lying
non-functional as of April 2000 due to lack of operation and maintenance fund
(i.e. shortage of cheque drawing power), drying up of sources, missing links due

to theft and wild fire ete. The department could not furnish status of these
schemes.

Inventory of assets created

departmental level. Physical verifications of these assets have not been carried
out. As per departmental record, cases of theft of 18148° metres of polypipes and
GI pipes from seven schemes occurred between March 1994 and December 1999
in two districts. The department stated that the loss was due to negligence,
irresponsibility and lack of sense of belongingness of the user community.

their performance and productivity level.

Planning

4.1.59 Under the programme, a socio-economic survey was required to be
conducted in towns/agglomerations having water supply, partial supply and no
supply so as to ascertain number of urban poor families requiring special attention
and for selection of economically viable projects. But the schemes were taken up
without conducting such survey. The communities to be served were not involved
or consulted in the planning, decision-making, construction and operation and
maintenance of the schemes. Reasons thereof were not stated.

PUBLIC WORKS DEPARTMENT |]

pest INTEGRATED AUDIT OF PUBLIC 7
DEPARTMENT INCLUDING MANPOWER
MANAGEMENT

The Public Works Department undertakes construction, improvement and
maintenance of roads and bridges as well as of Government residential and
non-residential buildings and construction of rural roads for village
connectivity through Additional Central Assistance under Basic Minimum
Service (BMS). Budgetary control was deficient in the Department leading
to failure in surrender of savings. BMS funds were diverted to non BMS
areas in violation of decision of the Planning Commission. Manpower
management was deficient leading to entertainment of excess staff besides
functioning of divisions without proper work load. Facilities for quality
testing were deficient. Machinery remained under-utilised.

Highlights

Against final provision of Rs.790.09 crore during 1995-96 to 2000-01 the
overall expenditure was Rs.696.52 crore. The savings ranged between 8 and
46 per cent under Revenue Section and 6 to 26 per cent under Capital
Section.

(Paragraph 4.2.5)

Unutilised provisions of Rs.14.57 crore were lying deposited under ‘8449-
Other Deposits’ as of March 2001 in contravention of the Financial Rules.

(Paragraph 4.2.7)

Sixteen road and bridge works taken up before 1995-96 remained incomplete
as of March 2001 even after expending Rs.4.32 crore as of March 2001.

(Paragraph 4.2.11)

Closure of 14 incomplete works (due for completion between March 1991
and March 1997) led the expenditure of Rs.1.69 crore incurred on them
unproductive.

(Paragraph 4.2.16)

Introduction

4.2.1. The Public Works Department (PWD), Manipur takes up all the
development works of construction and maintenance of roads and bridges in the
State other than Border Roads as well as construction and maintenance (including
internal electrification) of all Public Administrative buildings, rental housing and
other functional buildings of the Departments except those of Police Department
besides various deposit works.

4.2.3 The Principal Secretary (Works) redesignated (March 2001) as Additional
Chief Secretary Forest/Works is the Head of the Department and overall incharge.
The Chief Engineer (I) is the Head of Office. He is responsible for execution of
works under State Plan Schemes and Basic Minimum Services (BMS). The Chief
Engineer-II is responsible for other remaining works.

4.2.4 An integrated audit of the Department including manpower management
was taken up through test check of records for the period from 1995-96 to 2000-
2001 in the offices of both the Chief Engineers and eight'’ Executive Engineers
(out of 33) between April and June 2001 covering 52 per cent of the total
expenditure.

Delay in submission of monthly accounts

4.2.8 Divisional Officers are required to submit monthly accounts in complete
shape to the Accountant General (A&E) by the 10" of the following month.
Scrutiny, however, disclosed that during 1998-99 to 2000-2001 only 14 monthly
accounts were received within the due dates against 972 monthly accounts due
from 27 divisions for three years. Delay in submission of monthly accounts from
the remaining divisions ranged up to 121 days.

Construction of roads and bridges under State Plan/BMS

4.2.9 The budgeted outlay and expenditure of Department on the construction of
Roads and Bridges (Non-BMS) during the years 1995-96 to 2000-01 was
Rs.202.88 crore'* and Rs.197.79 crore’? respectively (departmental figures).

4.2.10 The targets and achievement on construction of roads and bridges
(departmental figures) are given in Appendix XXV. There was shortfall of 62 and
21 per cent in construction of roads under Basic Minimum Service and Other
District Roads respectively during 1996-97. The shortfall in construction of
bridges under State Highways was 67 per cent in 1997-98 and 33 per cent in
1998-99 and the shortfall under Major District Roads was 63 per cent in 1997-98.
No bridge work on inter village roads was done during 1996-97 to 2000-01.

Construction of residential and non-residential buildings

4.2.12 The budgeted outlay and the expenditure of the Department on the
construction of residential and non-residential buildings (departmental figures)
during 1995-96 to 2000-01 was Rs.46.90 crore? and Rs.43.43° crore™

4.2.13 The target and achievement in construction of residential and non-
residential Government buildings during 1995-96 to 2000-2001 (departmental
figures) are given in Appendix XXVI. Shortfall in construction of residential
buildings varied between 23 and 88 per cent while that of non-residential
buildings varied from 46 to 60 per cent. The Department (Financial Advisor)
attributed the reasons for shortfall to non-receipt of materials in time, site
condition and delayed commencement of the works.

Execution of works relating to roads and bridges

4.2.14 Excess expenditure over sanctioned estimates: Rules provide that where
the expenditure on a work, at any stage during execution is likely to exceed the
sanctioned estimate by 5 per cent in case of work costing more than Rs.5.00 lakh
and 10 per cent in case of work costing within Rs.5 lakh, revised estimate is
required to be got sanctioned from the competent authority.

4.2.15 It was however, noticed that on 57 works 4 Divisions” incurred total
expenditure of Rs.5.27 crore in excess of the admissible percentage over the
sanctioned estimates without having revised estimates sanctioned by the
competent authority. Reasons for incurring such unauthorised expenditure was
neither on record nor stated.

Unproductive expenditure

4.2.16 Test check of records of Tamenglong PW Division revealed that 14 road
works (detailed in Appendix XXVI/), due for completion between March 1991 and
March 1997 remained incomplete as of March 2001. It was seen that physical
achievement of these works ranged between 30 and 95 per cent and expenditure
of Rs.1.69 crore was incurred till March 1999, No expenditure was incurred on
these works during 1999-2000 and 2000-01. It was seen from the progress reports
of Executive Engineer of the Division to the Superintending Engineer that 12 out

Unplanned and defective issue of work orders

4.2.17 Khunutabi-Sansak road under Chandel PW Division was decided by the
Commissioner (Works) to be upgraded (July 1992) to Major District Road (MDR)
Standard. Accordingly, the work of improvement of Khunutabi-Sansak road via
Machi?’ was sanctioned (March 1993) for an estimated amount of Rs.35.70 lakh
The Executive Engineer issued work orders in piece-meal to 4 local contractors
between May 1995 and June 1995 for execution of earthwork in hill cutting and in
drains for 4 stretches” with the stipulation to complete the work between
December 1995 and August 1996, Reasons for issue of work orders in piece-meal
and non-finalisation of earth work in the remaining stretches (27.980 Km to
28.570 Km) as well as construction of RCC pipe culverts was, however, neither
on record nor stated. Earth work at stretches 0.00 km to 11.850 km and 11.850 km
to 18.710 Km were completed in April 1996 and August 1997 at a cost of
Rs.12.16 lakh and Rs.13.81 lakh respectively. The Division had not taken any
action for completion of the works at other stretches already awarded and for
finalisation of earthwork in the left out stretches as well as construction of RCC
pipe culverts (June 2001).

4.2.18 Thus, unplanned and defective issue of work orders rendered the
expenditure of Rs.25.97 lakh (Rs.12.16 lakh +Rs.13.81 lakh) unfruitful. Besides,
objective of improving the road to MDR standard remained unfulfilled even after
8 years of its sanction.

Basic Minimum Service operationalised by PWD
Shortfall in achievement under Basic Minimum Service (BMS)

4.2.19 The Government of India introduced (July 1996) the Basic Minimum
Services (BMS) Charter focusing on seven components with the broad objective
to ensure minimum infra structural facilities for improving the quality of life of all
sections of society. The components of the Programme inter alia provided for
connectivity to all villages and habitations by All Weather Roads (AWR). For
implementation of the schemes under BMS, the Government of India provided
Additional Central Assistance (ACA) of Rs.48 crore to the State Government
during 1996-97 to 2000-01 in the form of 90 per cent grant and 10 per cent loan.

4.2.20 For providing village connectivity under the BMS through all weather
roads the State Government allocated Rs.154.50 crore”® out of ACA to the PWD

Diversion of BMS outlays to Non-BMS areas

4.2.22 Contrary to the prescription of the Union Planning Commission, there
were following cases of diversion of BMS outlays to non-BMS as noticed in test-
check.

(i) The Principal Secretary (Works) had diverted Rs.3.35 crore during 1998-
99 from BMS provision for taking up new works of State Highways and Major
District Roads under State Plan due to financial constraints.

(ii) Expenditure of Rs.80.10 lakh incurred by the Churachandpur PW Division
in excess of sanctioned estimates on two major works under State Highways
(Rs.52.96 lakh) and one under Major District Roads (Rs.27.14 lakh) during 1998-
99 were booked (June 1999) under BMS works through transfer entries.

Management of Stores

4.2.23 Centralised procurement of major construction material (cement, steel,
bitumen ९८2.) are made by the Stores Division, PWD, Manipur on the basis of
previous year’s requirements of various working divisions. For this purpose,
allotment of funds for cheque drawal authority is issued by the Finance
Department from time to time. The materials are issued to the working divisions
against indents and receipt of advance payments. The office of the Executive
Engineer, Stores Division and the store adjoining the building were gutted by fire
on 27 January 2000. The records as well as the store material were reported to be
completely burnt and the loss was assessed by the EE, Stores Division to be
Rs.19.09 lakh. Departmental enquiry to assess the actual quantum of loss,
unaffected materials, if any, with value thereof had not been initiated (June 2001).

Accounts’ under the ‘Major Head-8658 Suspense Account’ for settlement of
transactions between PW Divisions for supplies made and services rendered

Physical verification of stores

4.2.25 Physical verification of stores was required to be conducted at least once
in a year. Scrutiny, however, revealed that the Stores Division had not conducted
the physical verification during 2000-01. Position in respect of other years was
not available as the records were stated to have been destroyed in fire incident.

Utilisation of Heavy Machinery

4.2.26 Procurement and utilisation of heavy machinery such as bulldozer, road-
roller, stone crusher etc. are controlled by the Mechanical Division-| of the
Department. Against requisitions of the working divisions, this Division issues the
machinery for utilisation in the works by the contractors on receipt of hire charges
raised through proforma invoice against the indenting divisions. The details of
utilisation of the working road rollers and bulldozers during 1997-98 to 2000-01
are given in Appendix XXVIII. The percentage of under-utilisation of road rollers
and bulldozers during the period ranged between 68 and 93, and 19 and 56
respectively.

4.2.27 Despite the bulldozers remaining underutilised, the Chief Engineer-II
hired bulldozer (between September 2000 and November 2000) from a private
party for departmental use and paid (October-November 2000) Rs.3.74 lakh as
hire-charge. Reasons for hiring private bulldozer in spite of departmental bull-
dozers remaining underutilised was neither on record nor stated.

4.2.28 Eight bull-dozers were lying unserviceable for periods ranging from one to
seven years. For repairing 5 dozers, although requisitions for procurement of
spare parts were submitted (May 2001) by the concerned Sub-Divisions, no action
was taken by the Mechanical Division in this regard. For the remaining 3 dozers,
although stated to be beyond economic repair, the Division had not taken any
action for their disposal through condemnation.

4.2.29 Out of 3 road rollers lying unserviceable beyond economic repair, survey
report against one roller was sent by the Mechanical Division (September 2000)
to the SE (HQ) for obtaining sanction for write off from the books of the Division.
Further action was awaited (June 2001). Action taken in respect of other 2 rollers
was neither on record nor stated.

es < ८2210 Works Expenditure

Quality control

4.2.30 Scrutiny of records of the Monitoring and Quality Control Division of the
Department and information furnished by them revealed that the number of
quality tests done by the Division annually declined from 677 in 1997-98 to 126
in 2000-01. The quality tests carried out by the Division were minor and executed
by crude methods as the test equipment were lying damaged and unserviceable. In
the absence of prescribed test, execution of works according to the standard
prescribed by the department could not be ensured.

Manpower Management
Sanctioned strength and Men-in-position

4.2.31 Category-wise position of sanctioned strength and men-in-position as on
31 March 2001 in the Department is given in Appendix XXIX. As against 2350
sanctioned strength, there were 4482 men-in-position. 1097 work charged staff on
completion of 10 years of service were converted from time to time into regular
establishment by the Chief Engineer-I as per instruction (April 1998) of the State
Government by creating supernumery posts.

Expenditure on excess staff in the Mechanical Division

4.2.32 The Mechanical Division No.1 was maintaining 155 excess staff over the
sanctioned strength. The Division incurred expenditure of Rs.2.46 crore
(calculated at the minimum of the time scale for 5 years only) on maintenance of
this excess staff during 1996-97 to 2000-01 as shown in Appendix XXX.

Excess expenditure on work charged and muster roll employees

4.2.33 Mention was made in paragraph 4.5.6 of the Report of the Comptroller
and Auditor General of India for the year ended 31 March 1998 regarding excess
expenditure of Rs.6.40 crore on work charged and muster roll employees. It was,
however, seen that against the ceiling of Rs.3.47 crore being 30 per cent of
maintenance allocation of Rs.11.58 crore during 1998-99 (Rs.4.02 crore), 1999-
2000 (Rs.4.05 crore) and 2000-01 (Rs.3.51 crore) the Department incurred an
expenditure of Rs.17.91 crore” on salaries/wages to the work charged and muster
roll employees during the period resulting in excess expenditure of Rs.14.44
crore.

Outstanding Standard License Fee

4.2.34 Position of realisation of Standard License Fee (SLF) and Furniture Rent
(FR) from the occupants of Government residential buildings in Imphal during
1995-96 to 2000-01 as obtained from Building Divisions No.I&II, PWD is given
in Appendix XXXI. The outstanding SLF and FR increased from Rs.0.69 crore at
the end of March 1996 to Rs.4.78 crore at the end of March 2001. The realisation

Recommendations
4,2.39 (i) Budgetary and expenditure control system is required to be improved

(ii) Proper planning for timely completion of the selected schemes according
to the availability of funds needs to be ensured to

( tii) Manpower management needs improvement and excess staff needs to be
profitably utilised. Necessity of maintaining huge muster-roll and work charged
staff needs to be reviewed and steps taken to utilise their services

(iv) Systems of quality control are required to be improved.

Procurement of underground cable by increasing 15 per cent of the actual

4.3.1. For laying underground cable at Khuman Lampak Sports Complex in

(October 1997 and July 1998) to a Guwahati based firm. Requirement of cable as

need and the requirement was rounded off to 8,400 metres. The supply orders
however were placed for a quantity of 8,500 metres for reasons not on record.
Against the supply orders, the Executive Engineer Sub-Station Construction
Division No.I received (December 1997 to December 1998) 8,545 metres of cable
and paid Rs.2.16 crore to the firm between February 1998 and March 2000.

Construction Division No.I revealed that the Division issued (February 1998 to
February 1999) 8041 metres cable to the Imphal Electrical Division No.1 for the
said work and the balance of 504 metres (cost: Rs.12.73 lakh) was lying
unutilised (August 2001) with the Division leading to locking up of funds worth
Rs12.73 lakh.

4.4.1. For construction of Bailey bridges at 10 different places of Churachandpur
District, the Additional Chief Engineer-I, PWD, Manipur placed (March 1997)
three supply orders to M/S Garden Reach Shipbuilders and Engineers (GRSE)
Ltd. (a Government of India undertaking), Calcutta for supply of Bailey bridge
components at a cost of Rs.2.43 crore. As per terms and conditions of the supply
orders 60 per cent of the value was to be paid by the consignee on receipt of the
proforma bill from the supplier and balance 40 per cent after receipt of materials.
The delivery was to be completed within 6 months from the date of receipt of
advance.

4.4.2 Test-check (December 2000) of records of the Executive Engineer,
Churachandpur Public Works Division revealed that the Division had paid (March
1997) Rs.2.40 crore by cheque as advance (98.77 per cent) to the GRSE but the
materials were not received by the Division within stipulated time (September
1997). 7

4.4.3 The Chief Engineer-I (CE-I), PWD, Manipur stated (5 September 2001)
that advance in excess was paid in order to procure bridge components speedily
for timely completion of the works and for utilising fund earmarked under Basic
Minimum Services as per decision of the Government. It was also stated that
bridge components valued at Rs.0.92 crore had been received between
November 1998 and June 2001 but their utilisation was not stated. CE-I however
further added that supply of the balance materials were affected due to ceasefire
agitation and blockade of roads.

4.4.4 Thus, the injudicious payment of advance to the supplier resulted in an
undue financial aid amounting to Rs.1.48 crore (Rs.2.40 crore- Rs.0.92 crore).

4.4.5 The matter was reported to Government (August 2001); reply had not
been received (December 2001).

45 Unproductive expenditure and extra liability on incomplete

Failure to complete the work even after a lapse of more than five years of the
rescission of contract rendered the expenditure of Rs.1.44_ crore
unproductive and led to extra liability of Rs.2.12 crore.

4.5.1 Mention was made in paragraph 4.6 of the Report of the Comptroller and
Auditor General of India for the year ended 31 March 1994 regarding payment of
unsecured mobilisation advance and undue financial benefit to contractor on the
works for reconstruction of Irilbung and Yairipok bridges on Imphal-Yairipok
road. It was, inter alia, mentioned that the firm discontinued the work from July
1992 and was paid Rs.1.10 crore up to ninth running account bill in March 1993
for the work done up to June 1992. Besides, the firm was paid Rs.18.11 lakh as
mobilisation advance of which Rs.15 lakh was outstanding. While discussing the
paragraph, the Public Accounts Committee (PAC) recommended that the latest
position of recovery of the advance be intimated to them. The actions taken by the
Department on the recommendation of the PAC are awaited.

4.5.2 Further test check (December 2000) of records of the Bridge Division,
PWD, Manipur revealed that the contract was rescinded in February 1996 and in
all an amount of Rs.1.50 crore was charged to the works as against tender value of
Rs.2.22 crore.

4.5.3 The Chief Engineer stated (September 2001) that Rs.6.26 lakh was
recovered by way of auction (Rs.1.45 lakh) and forfeiture (Rs.4.81 lakh) of
various construction material of the firm. For reconstruction of the bridges,
Government of India sanctioned (March 2000) Rs.2.78 crore out of Central Road
Fund as against the State Government’s proposal for Rs.2.90 crore.

4.5.4 But, the fact remains that works have not been started yet (November
2001) although work for Irilbung bridge (estimated cost Rs.1.60 crore) was
awarded to a new agency and for Yairipok bridge (estimated cost Rs.1.30 crore) it
was under process as stated. Further, total tentative expenditure on the two
projects would be Rs.4.34® crore against original tender value (Rs.2.22 crore).

4.5.5 Thus, failure to complete the work even after a lapse of more than five
years of the rescission of contract not only rendered the expenditure of Rs.1.44
crore (Rs.1.50 crore minus Rs.0.06 crore) unproductive but led to extra liability to
the tune of Rs.2.12 crore (Rs.4.34 crore minus Rs.2.22 crore) on the two projects.

4.5.6 The matter was reported to Government (April 2001); reply had not been
received (December 2001).

Purchase beyond requirement led to locking up of funds amounting to
Rs.88.30 lakh and interest loss of Rs.21 lakh to the Government

5.1.1 According to general principles of purchase laid down in Section 38 of
Central Public Works Department Manual, Volume II, stores should not be
purchased in excess and much in advance of requirement.

5.1.2 Test-check (February 2001) of records of the Executive Engineer, Project
Stores Division, Imphal revealed that the Division did not have any prescribed
reserve stock limit but without assessing the requirements purchased 20,996 Disc
Insulators (without Hardware) valued Rs.82.74 lakh between November 1998 and
May 1999 and 21,000 Tension Hardware fittings for Disc Insulators valued
Rs.55.15 lakh between March 1999 and September 2000 for stock. Of these, only
10,106! Disc Insulators (without Hardware) and 3,716! Tension Hardware fittings
for Disc Insulators could be issued to the executing divisions up to the end of
January 2001 leaving a balance of 10,890 Disc Insulators (without Hardware) and
17,284 Tension Hardware fittings for Disc Insulators valued Rs.42.91 lakh and
Rs.45.39 lakh respectively. Considering the average monthly issues of 388 Disc
Insulators and 186 Tension Hardware fittings, the balance stock materials would
meet the requirements of the Division for another 28 and 93 months respectively.

5.1.3 Thus, purchase without assessing the requirement rendered unnecessary
locking up of funds of Rs.88.30 lakh. Besides there was loss of interest to the tune
of Rs.20.01 lakh” to the Government on the locked up fund which could have
been utilised for other development activities.

5.2.1 Mention was made in paragraph 5.1.8 of the Report of the Comptroller
and Auditor General of India for the year ended 31 March 1998 regarding locking

5.2.2 Test-check in audit (December 2000) of records of water supply stores
(Urban) Division, Imphal revealed further cases of non-utilisation of materials as
detailed below:-

5.2.3. The Division, without assessing the actual requirement had procured
(1996-97) 7688 Running metres (RM) of Ductile Iron (DI) pipes with cement
Mortar Lining of 4 specifications (250mm to 500 mm dia) at a cost of Rs.158.38
lakh from a Calcutta based firm at DGS&D rates as per supply order (July 1996)
of the Chief Engineer (CE), Urban. Out of this, 5194.50 RMs of DI pipes were
issued (July 1997 to November 2000) to two Divisions on the basis of their
indents leaving balance of 2493.50 RMs pipes valued at Rs.38.93 lakh unutilised
in stock as of November 2000. Of which, utilisation of 250 mm dia DI pipes was
only 29 per cent during the five years ending 31 March 2001.

5.2.4 Between January 1999 and August 2000, the Division also procured 248
number of CI specials. (CI sluice valve of five specifications - 206 number and
CI non-returnable valve of three specifications- 42 number) valued at Rs.43.75
lakh from local suppliers without assessing the estimated requirements, while it
had 238 number of identical items (CI sluice valve 222 number and CI non-
returnable valve -16 number) already in stock as on 1 April 1998, During 1996-97
to 2000-01, 126 number of CI sluice valve of five specifications only were issued
from stock which ranged between 14 to 33 per cent of total stock leaving a
balance of materials valuing Rs.50.87 lakh (March 2001).

5.2.5 Similarly, the Division had procured (March 1999- March 2000) 42
number of CI fittings of seven specifications valued at Rs.8.05 lakh, of which no
item was issued from stock up to March 2001.

5.2.6 In reply, the Chief Engineer stated (August 2001) that materials were

procured after assessing the requirement of replacement of old pipes of various

schemes sanctioned during 1995-96 and these would be utilized while carrying
121



Audit Report forthe year ond I I आल भ थक Report for the year ended 31 March 2001

out the work of replacement and maintenance works. Reply of the CE is not
tenable because the Department did not prepare any work plan/estimate fof,
carrying out replacement/maintenance of the works sanctioned during 1995-96
nor had received any deposit for execution of deposit works during the coming
years. Hence, possibility of utilising the material is remote.

5.2.7. Thus, the action of the Department in procuring the materials without
assessment of requirement was not only injudicious but resulted in locking up of
fund to the tune of Rs.97.85 lakh. Besides there was loss of interest of Rs.54.46
lakh? to the Government on the locked up fund calculated at the average
borrowing rate.

-CHAPTER VI

FINANCIAL ASSISTANCE TO LOCAL BODIES AND
_ OTHERS

6.1.1 Autonomous bodies and authorities are set up to discharge generally
non- commercial functions in the nature of public utility services. These
bodies/authorities by and large receive substantial financial assistance from the
Government. Government also provides substantial financial assistance to other
institutions such as those registered under the respective state Co-operative
Societies Act, Companies Act, 1956 etc. to implement certain programme of the
State Government. The grants are intended essentially for maintenance of
educational institutions, hospitals, charitable institutions, construction and
maintenance of schools and hospital buildings, improvement of roads and other
communication facilities under municipalities and local bodies.

6.1.2 During 2000-01 financial assistance of Rs.22.63 crore was paid to
various autonomous bodies and others grouped as under—
