SECTION ‘A’— REVIEW

FOREST AND ENVIRONMENT DEP

MANIPUR POLLUTION CONTROL BOARD

Manipur Pollution Control Board established for containing the vehicular
and industrial pollution and solid waste management had not conducted
impact analysis after the consented units (183) had gone into productive
operation. In the State, vehicular emission tests were conducted only in
Imphal city and 48681 vehicles registered in other seven districts remained
uncovered under the emission testing exercise. The Board had not assessed
carbon monoxide levels in the State due to non-procurement of the
equipment. The quantum of wastes generated from 57 medical units in
Imphal city was not assessed. Imphal Municipal Council unauthorisedly
dumped bulk of the wastes in an unprotected plot at Lamphelpat. The city
wastes were also dumped in the other low lying areas, roadsides and river
banks. Thus purpose of establishing the Board largely remained unfulfilled
despite expenditure of Rs.1.24 crore incurred during 1996 to 2001.

Introduction

6.6.1 In recognition of the need for environmental protection various
regulatory and promotional measures have been taken in the country. These
include enactment of the Air (Prevention and Control of Pollution) Act, 1981 and
Environment (Protection) Act, 1986 under which the Biomedical Waste
(Management and Handling) Rules, 1998 and the Municipal Solid Waste
(Management and Handling) Rules, 2000 were made by the Government of India.
But Air (Prevention and Control of Pollution) Rules, 1983 has not been notified in
the State Gazette (November 2001). Reasons for delay was not stated. All these
Acts and Rules extend to the State of Manipur. In March 1996 the entire state was
declared an air pollution control area. For implementing these above Acts and
Rules Government of Manipur established the Environment and Ecology Wing
(EEW) under the Forest and Environment Department(1997) and Manipur
Pollution Control Board (MPCB) (1989). State Transport authorities are also
involved in smoke emission tests of vehicles. हि

1996-97 to 2000-2001 were test checked during April/May 2001.

through Central Pollution Control Board (CPCB) and the State Government and
fees from consent certificates and smoke emission tests.

6.6.5 Out of total savings of Rs.2.90 lakh under grants from GOI, the Board
utilised funds of Rs.1.43 lakh (Rs.110.34 lakh minus Rs.108.91 lakh) to make
expenditure on components to be met out of State fund resulting in overall
savings of Rs.1.47 lakh only. In the absence of information, the purpose for which
the Central fund was diverted and utilised by the Board on components to be
funded by the State Government could not be verified in audit. Information on
submission of utilization certificates by the Board to GOI was not found on
record. Against grants of Rs.104.69 lakh from Government of Manipur (GOM),
the Board furnished utilisation certificates for Rs.54.27 lakh only.

Source of Pollution

6.6.6 As per EEW, the major sources of air pollution in the state are smoke
emission from unmaintained vehicles, use of adulterated vehicle fuel, open
burning of Urban Solid wastes, Stone crushing units, Brick fields etc. However,
the number of such polluting units was not made available.

Consent management

6.6.7 (a) In exercise of power conferred under Section 21 of Air Act, 1981,
MPCB granted consent to 183 industrial units (Appendix XXXV) during 1996-
2001 for operating plants in the State. The Board did not prescribe/approve
pollution control equipments for installation and operation in the premises of the
industry as envisaged in the Act. The Board statéd (May 2001) that considering
the nature of industries and level of air pollution, installation of pollution control
equipment was considered not necessary. Statement of the Board contradicted the
assessment made by EEW that smoke emission from Stone crushing units and
Brick fields are one of the major source of air pollution in the State.

(b) Section 17 (I) of the Air Act empowers the Board to conduct
inspections at all reasonable times, in the industrial units and their pollution
control equipment. But the Board did not conduct inspections at regular intervals
in the industrial units (183) for which consent certificates were issued. The Board
stated (May 2001) that the inspection was done whenever necessary or when there

The number of tests conducted were totally inadequate (only 3 per cent) to

(b) Vehicles declared unfit in the emission tests were to report for recheck
within 15 days after doing the recommended repair. But 767 vehicles declared
unfit during 1996-97 to 2000-2001 did not turn up for the required check. There
was nothing on record to indicate that these vehicles had been barred from plying.

(c) Automobile emission tests had never been conducted in the other
districts of the State due to paucity of funds. Resultantly, 48,681 vehicles
registered in these districts during 1996-2001 (1996: 8691, 1997: 9311, 1998:
9738, 1999: 10176, 2000: 10765) totally remained uncovered under the emission
testing exercise.

Implementation of projects funded by the Government of India
(a) Ambient Carbon Monoxide Monitoring in Imphal city

6.6.9 The Government of India sanctioned (August 1997) Rs.5.54 lakh to the
MPCB for this project intended to determine the ambient Carbon Monoxide (CO)
in Imphal city and the first instalment of Rs.2.77 lakh was released in August
1997. Test-check of records revealed that the requisite equipment (Non-dispersive
Carbon Monoxide Analyser) was not purchased (May 2001) and three
appointments were made as against two and continued for 2% years (May 1997 to

128


eM Financial Assistance to Local Bodies and Others VI Financial Assistance to Local Bodies and Others

October 1999), The expenditure of Rs. 1.77 lakh on salary exceeded the
sanctioned amount (Rs.0.84 lakh) by Rs.0.93 lakh.

6.6.10 The Board stated (May 2001) that they had executed the project without
the said equipment and as the instrument was not there, theoretical analysis of CO
level was carried out when the smoke emission test was conducted. This is not
tenable as smoke emission test conducted with the help of Auto Exhaust Analyser
can only measure the extent of pollution of the vehicle.

6.6.11 Although the Board has furnished (May 2000) progress report, the
second instalment of Rs.2.77 lakh was yet to be received (May 2001).

(b) Air Laboratory for strengthening Manipur Pollution Control Board

6.6.12 | The Government of India sanctioned (June 1997) Rs.2.50 lakh for
purchase of one “Auto Analyser for measuring petrol vehicles” (Rs.1.30 lakh) and
one “Stack Monitoring Kits to survey for emission of brick fields” (Rs.1.20 lakh)
under this project and released (June 1997) Rs.1.20 lakh as the first instalment
during 1997-98. The Board had procured the Auto Analyser at a cost of Rs.1.13
lakh and used it for automobile emission tests. Although Government of India had
released the second instalment of Rs.1.30 lakh in March 2000, the Stack
Monitoring Kit was not purchased (May 2001), in absence of which monitoring of
emissions from brick field chimneys (54) remained uncovered.

Wastes Management
(a) Source of wastes

6.6.13 In the State, wastes from domestic sewage are the major sources of
solid wastes and the wastes from the hospitals, clinics, etc. constitute the source of
biomedical wastes. Responsibilities for the management and handling of these
wastes vests in the Municipalities.

(b) Identification/Inventorisation of units

6.6.14 Bio-Medical waste generated by 57 medical units (Hospitals: 9; Clinics:
30; Primary Health Centre: 18) in Imphal City was not assessed. The MPCB
stated (May 2001) that assessment of the quantum of wastes generated from these
hospitals/clinics was under process.

(० Storage and disposal of wastes

6.6.15 One dumping site at Lamdeng Khunou under Imphal Municipal
Council (IMC) was identified by MPCB. But dumping of wastes in the site had
not yet been started (May 2001). According to a report of the MPCB the IMC
unauthorisedly dumped bulk of the wastes in a plot at Lamphelpat which is not
protected by boundary walls or guarded against trespassers. The city wastes were
also dumped in the other low lying areas, road sides and the river banks. The
dumping was erratic, disorderly and was not in conformity with the compliance
criteria of Schedule II & III of the Municipal Solid Wastes (Management and
Handling) Rules, 2000. Resultantly, the entire surroundings were facing serious
129



uit Report for the er ne 3 MI न नननननभन---त& Report for the year ended 31 March 2001
obnoxious smell from the rotting solid wastes polluting the air. No survey was
conducted by the Board to assess the extent of adverse effects on the health and.

environment caused due to unsystematic disposal for wastes.

(d) Segregation of wastes

6.6.16  Bio-medical waste needed to be segregated into containers, bags at the
point of generation prior to storage, transportation, treatment and disposal. But

this had not been introduced as yet (May 2001).

Monitoring and Evaluation

6.6.17 The performance of the Board was never evaluated/reviewed by any
internal or external agency.

6.6.18 | The above points were referred to Government (August 2001); reply
had not been received (December 2001).

Recommendations

6.6.19 | The EEW/Board should initiate effective steps for stoppage of dumping
of wastes in the unprotected places, roadsides and river banks. Steps should also
be taken for segregation of wastes at the point of generation before transportation
and disposal.

SECTION कफ! PARAGRAPHS

Purchase of CGI sheets at a higher rate than the Government approved rate
led to an avoidable extra expenditure of Rs.20.69 lakh

6.7.1 For a unified system of purchasing of CGI sheets for ‘Indira Awas
Yojana’ (IAY) in the State, the Development Department directed (June 2000) all
the Deputy Commissioners (DCs) to place indents with the authorised dealer
based at Imphal. The approved rate for purchase of CGI sheets from the
authorised dealer was Rs.1996 per bundle (thickness: 0.40 mm; length: 9 feet
each). .

6.7.2 Test-check (January 2001) of records of the District Rural Development
Agency (DRDA), Senapati revealed that instead of purchasing CGI sheets from
the authorised dealer, the DC purchased (between August and September 2000)
1751 bundles of CGI sheets (cost: Rs.52.53 lakh) at the lowest tendered rate of
Rs.3000 per bundle by placing supply order (July 2000) with a Senapati based
firm. The DC, by inviting tenders in October 2000, further purchased 310 bundles
of CGI sheets (cost: Rs.9.30 lakh) at the lowest rate of Rs.3000 per bundle from
an Imphal based firm (weight/length of the supplied quantities of CGI sheets was
neither on record nor stated).

6.7.3 Thus, as a result of purchase of 2061 bundles of CGI sheets at a rate
higher than the rate approved by the Government, there was an avoidable extra
expenditure of Rs.20.69 lakh.

6.7.4 The matter was reported to department/Government (July 2001); replies
had not been received (December 2001).

ury Rules prohibits drawal of money from the

6.8.2 Test-check (February 2001) of records of the Autonomous District
Council (ADC), Tamenglong revealed that the State Government sanctioned
(March 2000) Rs.35.56 lakh as grants-in-aid to the ADC, Tamenglong for
construction of 12 Primary School buildings with a stipulation to deposit 50 per
cent of the amount under the Major Head-Other Deposits. The Chief Executive
Officer of the Tamenglong ADC drew (March 2000) the amount and deposited
(March 2000) Rs.17.78 lakh into the treasury under 8449-Other Deposits. The
amount was lying under the deposit head (June 2001) frustrating the very purpose
for which it was sanctioned.

6.8.3 The matter was reported to Government (September 2001); reply had
not been received (December 2001).

tequired to furnish replies to the Inspection Reports through the respective heads

Audit Report for the year ended 31 Mar 20 Report for the year ended 31 March 2001

SECTION ‘A’ - REVIEW

Highlights

Though there has been a steady growth of registration of vehicles during
1996-97 to 2000-01, the realisation of taxes/revenues therefrom has shown an

erratic trend.
(Paragraph 7.5.9)

The Department failed to impose penalty of Rs.15.17 lakh on vehicles which
violated provisions of the Acts and Rules.

(Paragraphs 7.5.11 and 7.5.14)

In the absence of departmental weigh bridge the violation of permissible
weight limits remained undetected.

(Paragraph 7.5.12)

No system existed in the Department to watch receipt of drafts sent for
revalidation. Thus, 187 bank drafts for Rs.2.64 lakh remained unaccounted

for.

(Paragraph 7.5.20)

Though the Manipur State Road Transport Corporation had defaulted in
payment of taxes to the tune of Rs.11.86 lakh, no action was initiated against
it.

(Paragraph 7.5.28)

Introduction

7.5.1 The conditions for plying of motor vehicles and levy and collection of
taxes thereon are governed by the provisions of the Motor Vehicles Act, 1988,
Central Motor Vehicles Rules 1989, Assam Motor Vehicles Taxation Act, 1936
(as adopted by Manipur), Manipur Motor Vehicles Rules, 1951, and the Manipur
Motor Vehicles Act, 1998. The Acts and Rules empower the Transport
Department to levy and collect road tax, registration fee, inspection fee, permit
fee, composite fee etc.

138


eH Revenue Receipts VII Revenue Receipts

Organisational set-up

7.5.2 The Director of Transport is the head of the Department and is assisted
by two Deputy Directors of Transport at headquarters and six? District Transport
Officers (DTOs) at the field level. However, functions of the Chairman and the
Secretary of the State Transport Authority (STA) are carried out by the
Commissioner/Secretary Transport and the Director of Transport respectively. In
his capacity as Secretary of the STA, the Director of Transport is responsible for
the grant and realisation of fees for national permit, tourist permit, stage carriage

permit and contract carriage permit as specified in the Motor Vehicles Acts and
Rules framed thereunder.

Scope of audit

7.5.3 A review on receipts under Taxes on Vehicles for the period from
1995-96 to 2000-01 was conducted with a view to examine the effectiveness and
adequacy of the rules and procedures, assessment and collection of taxes, and fees
and fines due thereof. For this purpose, the records of the Director of Transport

and two DTOs (Imphal West and Kangpokpi) were test checked between June
and July 2001.

Trend of revenue

7.5.4 Taxes on Vehicles are one of the major sources of tax revenue for the
Government of Manipur. The Budget estimates/targets and actual collection of
revenue for the years 1995-96 to 2000-01 were as under—

Table No. 7.6

of tax/fees with the introduction of Manipur Motor Vehicles Taxation Act, 1998
(May 1999).

EEN लि कि 9.2... ८५ ८॥ ८... /35<....4.455.2 2.4 VI Revenue Receipts
rr
System defects

Non-levy of penalty on over loading of vehicles

7.5.10 Section 194 (1) of Motor Vehicles Act, 1988 read with its Section 113
provides that whoever drives a motor vehicle carrying goods in excess of
Permissible weight shall be penalised. The Government of Manipur vide a
Notification dated 26 September 1981 laid down the maximum weight of 10 tons
that a goods vehicle could carry and by another notification dated 18 July 1989 a
penalty ranging from Rs.100 to Rs.4000 could be imposed for first and
subsequent offences for carrying of goods beyond permissible weights.

7.5.11 8 cross check (July 2001) of the Goods Movement Register of
Taxation check post at Kangpokpi revealed that during April-May 1996 and
January 1998 to June 1999 transport vehicles numbering 880 carried goods in
excess of the limit prescribed for which a penalty of Rs.12.12 lakh was leviable.
The Department, however, failed to impose penalty on these vehicles resulting in
non-levy of penalty of Rs.12.12 lakh. हु

7.5.12 The Department did not have any weigh-bridge either at the two check
gates at Tadubi in Senapati District and Sekmai in Imphal West District or at any
other location. Thus, there was no effective and foolproof means to verify the
weight of the goods carried by the vehicles. This lacunae resulted in plying of
goods vehicles carrying goods beyond the prescribed limit, set by the
Government, not being detected.

7.5.13 Under Section 192-A of the Motor Vehicles Act,1988 read with
Government of Manipur, Transport Department Notification (18 July 1989)
whoever drives a motor vehicle without a valid permit shall be punishable with a
fine ranging from Rs.500 to Rs.2000 for the first and subsequent offences.

7.5.14 6 test check (July 2001) of the records of the Directorate of Transport
and DTO, Imphal West and Kangpokpi revealed that in 489 cases (1995-96: 13;
1996-97: 67; 1997-98: 78; 1998-99: 96; 1999-2000: 138; and 2000-01: 97),
penalty of Rs.3.05 lakh was not imposed for non-obtaining permit during 1995-96
to 2000-01.

Non-assignment of new Registration mark

7.5.15 According to Section 47 of the Motor Vehicles Act, 1988 and
Notification dated 31 January 2000 of Government of India (Ministry of Surface
Transport, New Delhi), when a motor vehicle, registered in one State has been
kept in another State for a period exceeding twelve (12) months, the owner of the
vehicle shall apply to the Registering Authority for assignment of a new
registration mark, failing which the owner shall be punishable with a fine of one
hundred rupees besides fees for the assignment of the new registration mark.

7.5.16 Test-check (July 2001) of the records of DTO, Imphal West revealed
that 779 vehicles (1990-91: 45; 1991-92: 11. 1992-93: 38; 1993-94: 73; 1994-95:
76: 1995-96: 121: 1996-97: 70; 1997-98: 65; 1998-99: 81; 1999-2000: 197; 2000-

141


uit Report for the ee I Report for the year ended 31 March 2001

01: 02)were kept in the State for more than 12 months but the owners re: ea
applied for new assignment mark nor had the Registering Authority int wi any
action to assign new registration marks, This failure resulted in in isation of
Registration fees totalling Rs.0.80 lakh and a penalty of Rs.0.78 lakh.

Non-realisation of Inspection Fee

7.8.17 Under Section 56 of the Motor Vehicles Act, 1988 and Rule 62 of
Central Motor Vehicle Rules, 1989 a transport vehicle shall not be deemed to be
validly registered unless it carries a certificate of fitness issued हा हि
Department against payment of inspection fees. Before issue of such certificate,
the Motor Vehicles Inspector shall inspect the vehicle against payment of
inspection fees. The certificate so issued in respect of the new vehicles shall
remain valid for a period of two years after which the same 1s required to be
renewed every year.

7.5.18  Test-check of “Combined registers” and “Fitness Certificate Registers”
of two DTOs’ revealed (July 2001) that in 698 cases required to be renewed
during the different periods falling between November 1995 and September 2000,
the Department did not initiate action for renewal of fitness certificates. Failure to
renew fitness certificates resulted in non-realisation of inspection fee of Rs.1.02
lakh.

Non-accountal of receipt, issue and disposal of bank drafts

7.5.19 As per Central Treasury Rules, all demand drafts as and when received
should be entered in a Register of valuables and their disposal is to be watched.

7.5.20 Test-check (July 2001) of the records of the STA, Manipur revealed
that during the period from August 1995 to February 2001, 187 bank drafts for
Rs.2.64 lakh were sent back to the issuing banks/STA of other States for
revalidation. But there was no monitoring mechanism in place to watch that these
drafts were received back and deposited into Government account.

Non-levy/short-realisation of authorisation/permit and composite fee and non
maintenance of proper records

7.5.21 Under Rule 87 of the Central Motor Vehicles Rules, 1989 authorisation
fee of Rs.500 per annum was fixed for grant of a National Permit to the owner of
home State vehicles. Section 81(2) of the Central Motor Vehicles Act, 1988 lays
down that a permit may be renewed on an application made not less than 15 days
before the date of iis capiry Test check (July 2001) of records of the STA,
Manipur. revealed that permits in respect of six (6) trucks were not renewed (July
2001) despite the fact that the period of authorisation had expired. The truck
owners did not even surrender their permits as a proof that the vehicles were
inoperative. Due to non-renewal of the said permits not only the authorisation fee
and the permit fee of Rs.0.07 lakh remained unrealised but also composite fee

“Imphal West and Kangpokpi.

142


Chapter ४71 Revenue Receipts
—_—_—_—_—_—_—_—_—_—_—_—_— कक कक कस उस अपसअपरअरअरकञ

totalling Rs.1.05 lakh due to other States covered by the National Permit remained
uncollected and unremitted.

7.5.22 Again, the Transport Department under Notification dated 26 May 1994
prescribed the rate of composite fee of Rs.3000 annually with effect from January
1994, in respect of public carrier goods vehicles belonging to other States and
Union Territories plying in the State of Manipur under National Permits granted
by the other States/Union Territories. Test check (July 2001) of records of the
STA, Manipur revealed that in case of seventeen (17) vehicles there was short
realisation of composite fees amounting to Rs.0.20 lakh.

7.5.23 In order to keep a watch over the demand, recovery, computation of
arrears, the composite fee actually received and the follow up action for
realisation of composite fee due from other States in respect of motor vehicles
covered by National Permits, it is necessary to have complete details of all the
Permits issued from time to time by other States for operating the vehicles in the
State of Manipur. The information regarding the National Permits issued by other
States for plying of vehicles in Manipur was not available with the STA. In the
absence of these basic information, the composite fee due from other States, but
not received could neither be determined nor could any action be taken for its
recovery by the STA.

7.5.24 Under the Central Treasury Rules, after the head of office makes any
remittance of revenue, he should, as soon as possible, after the end of the month,
obtain from the Treasury and Bank a consolidated receipt record of all remittances
made during the month and verify the same with the entries made in the
Remittance Register/Bank Draft Register for ascertaining the correctness of
deposit into the Government account.

7.5.25 Test check of Bank Draft Register of the STA, Manipur revealed (July
2001) that 1939 bank drafts totalling Rs.39.83 lakh were deposited into the
Treasury/Bank for credit into the Government account during April 1995 to
March 2001. However, the entries made in that Register were neither reconciled
with that of the Treasury and Bank figures nor the copies of Treasury Receipt
schedule or Bank Scrolls obtained during the entire period covered by the review.

7.5.26 Similarly, it was noticed (July 2001) that neither the DTO, Imphal West
nor the DTO, Kangpokpi reconciled the figures of revenue deposited through
challans with those of the Treasury during the entire period covered in review. As
a result, the possibility of misclassification/misappropriation of Government
revenue could not be ruled out.

Other points
Non-initiation of action against defaulter

7.5.27. The Assam Motor Vehicles Taxation Act, 1936(extended to the State of
Manipur) and the Manipur Passenger and Goods Taxation Act, 1978 and the
Rules, 1979 lay down the action to be taken against defaulters of motor vehicles

taxes.

~ हु 143


Ault Report forthe a I 5 +म+++«मन++«+33 ended 31 March 2001

Audit Report for the

7.5.28 Scrutiny (July 2001) of records revealed that payment of taxes of
Rs.11.86 lakh as on 31 March 2001 was due from the General Manager, MeP*y,
State Road Transport Corporation. However, no demand notice was issued (July
2001). This inaction of the Department thus resulted in non-realisation of revenue
to the tune of Rs. 11.86 lakh.

Internal audit

7.5.29 The Department had no Internal Audit Wing. As such, no proper checks
were exercised on effective application and administration of the Acts and Rules
and related administrative instructions in order to enhance the collection of
revenue by blocking loopholes.

Monitoring and evaluation

7.5.30 | The Department did not have a monitoring and evaluation cell. It was
stated (July 2001) that the system of monitoring and evaluation was carried out by
the Directorate. However, no reports of the same could be furnished to Audit for.
verification.

Recommendations

7.5.31(i) Measures may be adopted for procurement and installation of weigh
bridges at strategic locations so as to detect and penalise vehicles carrying weights
beyond the prescribed limit. Internal Audit Wing and an independent Monitoring
and Evaluation Cell may be set up to strengthen the enforcement/application of
the provisions of the Acts and Rules which, in turn, would help in boosting up the
revenue.

(ii) At the Directorate level, records may be maintained in proper formats
so as to facilitate easy detection of tax evaders and to watch receipt and deposit of
various fees.

(iii) Efforts may be made to minimise the cost of collection.

SECTION ‘B’ PARAGRAPHS

[| POWER DEPARTMENT :] DEPARTMENT के

[7.6 Short-realisation of revenue due toless billing =| Short-realisation of revenue due to less billing

Agee acres ie. Hille pee eon tes of incorrect rates of billing on account of defective meters
| resulted in short-realisation of revenue of Rs.3.48 lakh

7.6.1 As per the tariff rates applicable the minimum monthly charges
prescribed for bulk supply are Rs.228 per KW of contract demand per month plus
demand charges at the rate of Rs.64 per KW of 60 per cent of the contract
demand per month. Where supply to the consumer has been given without a meter
or where the meter fixed is found defective the consumer shall pay the flat rate of
Rs.380 per KW of the contract demand per month as energy charges plus demand
charges. In case of public water works where the connected load is less than 100
KW per month and where the meter is found defective the consumer shall pay flat
rate of Rs.342 per KW of the connected load per month and where the connected
load is above 100 KW the consumer shall pay the flat rate of Rs.64 per KW of 60
per cent of the contract demand per month.

7.6.2 Test-check of records (January-February 2001) of the Executive
Engineer, Imphal Electricity Division No. III, Imphal revealed that the Division
levied energy charges of Rs.12.13 lakh instead of Rs.15.60 lakh due to application
of incorrect rates of billing charges on account of defective meters which resulted
in short realisation of revenue of Rs.3.48 lakh as detailed in Appendix XXXVI.

7.6.3 The matter was reported to the department/Government (July 2001);
replies had not been received (December 2001).

TAXATION DEPARTMENT

[7.7__ Evasion of tax due to suppression of purchase turnover __| Evasion of tax due to suppression of purchase turnover

Concealment of purchase turnover led to evasion of tax of Rs.2.56 lakh and
penalty of Rs.3.83 lakh

1.71 The Manipur Sales Tax Act, 1990 provides that every dealer liable to
pay tax under any of the earlier laws, shall be liable to pay tax under this Act on
all sales or purchases of goods other than goods specified in Schedule III. The Act
further provides that if any dealer conceals the particulars or deliberately
furnishes inaccurate particulars, the Commissioner may direct that such dealer
shall pay by way of penalty, in addition to the tax payable, a sum not exceeding
one and a half times of that amount.

145



(Audit Report for the ye or or I अीननिकिकीकीलककक कक अल Report for the year ended 31 March 2001

7.7.2 Test-check of records of the Commissioner of Taxes, Manipur revealed
(August - September 2000) that assessments of three dealers of ‘Liquetieg
Petroleum Gas (LPG) were finalised during April 1999 to July 2000 on the gross
turn over of Rs.99.53 lakh as per returns furnished by the dealers during
December 1988 to March 2000.

7.73 Cross-check of records (ST 48) maintained by the taxation check Post
at Kangpokpi, however, revealed that the dealers had imported LPG valued
Rs. 167.43 lakh during the above period.

1.14 The concealment of turn over of Rs.67.90 lakh resulted in evasion of
tax of Rs.5.01 lakh, besides maximum leviable penalty of Rs.7.52 lakh, as
detailed in the Appendix XXXVI.

115 On being reported, the Commissioner of Taxes stated (June 2001) that
the dealers had been re-assessed and demand notices for tax as well as penalty
were issued (May 2001). The Department subsequently intimated (August 2001)
that one of the three dealers had paid the re-assessed sales tax but the other two
dealers were defaulters from whom Sales Tax of Rs.2.56 lakh and penalty of
Rs.3.83 lakh were due.

7.7.6 The matter was referred to Government (July 2001), reply had not been
received (December 2001).

7.8. Short levy of Central Sales Tax

Assessment of tax at concessional rate instead of the appropriate rate
without supported by prescribed declaration form resulted in short-levy of
CST of Rs.3.71 lakh

7.8.1 Under the provision of Central Sales Tax Act, 1956 and the Central
Sales Tax (Registration and Turnover) Rules, 1957 tax is leviable on inter State
sale of goods other than declared goods at the rate of 10 per cent or at the rate
applicable to the sale or purchase of such goods within the State, whichever is
higher. However tax at the concessional rate of 4 per cent is leviable if such sales
of goods are covered by valid declarations in Form “C” issued by the purchasing
dealers.

7.8.2 A test check (October 2000) of records of the Superintendent of Taxes,
Moreh revealed that a dealer was assessed (August 1999) to tax at the rate of 4 per
cent for inter State sales valued at Rs.61.91 lakh made by him. Since the sales
were not supported by the prescribed declaration forms (Form “C”), the dealer

was liable to pay tax at the rate of 10 per cent. This resulted a short levy of
Rs.3.71 lakh.

7.8.3 The department stated (September 2001) that the declaration in form

“C” had not been furnished by the dealer and the assessment was made by the

assessing authority at the concessional rate of 4 per cent instead of 10 the concessional rate of 4 per cent instead of 10 per cent.
146



ree I Revenue Receipts Chapter VII Revenue Receipts

However, the dealer had been re-assessed (March 2001) and directed to pay
Rs.3.60 lakh (Rs.6.19 lakh: 10 per cent of Rs.61.91 lakh: Rs.2.59 lakh already
Paid) failing which recovery certificate would be issued to the Deputy Collector
concerned for recovery of the same as arrear of land revenue. The reason for
raising short demand of Rs.10,000 had not been stated by the department. But the
actual position of recovery of tax is still awaited (October 2001).

7.8.4 The matter was reported to Government (May 2001); reply has not been
received (December 2001),

‘7.9 1,055 of revenue due to irregular cancellation of certificate of
registration

[Reena Rg a on time sad in] of certificate of registration in time resulted in loss of
revenue of Rs.1.79 lakh

7.9.1 Section 29 of the Manipur Sales Tax Act, 1990 lays down that when a
certificate of registration is cancelled due to closure or dissolution of a firm the
dealer shall be liable to pay tax on his stock of goods remaining unsold at the time
of cancellation of the certificate.

7.9.2 Test-check (August-September 2000) of the assessment records of the
Superintendent of Taxes, Zone IV, Imphal revealed that registration certificate of
a dealer was cancelled with effect from 1 October 1998 on the basis of application
(10 March 1999). But the assessing authority did not levy tax leviable on the
closing stock of goods valued at Rs.25.52 lakh as on 1 October 1998. This
resulted in loss of revenue of Rs.1.79 lakh.

7.9.3 The Department, however, (September 2001) stated that the assessing
authority had not processed the cancellation of the registration and in the mean
time the dealer abandoned his place of business at Imphal after selling the plot
and building where he ran his business. The reply is not tenable since the
responsibility of dealing with the application to cancel registration number in time
and to levy of tax on the closing stock of the dealer devolved on the assessing

authority.

7.9.4 The matter was referred to Government (May 2001); reply had not been
received (December 2001).

A Rf वक्‍ृफदकदकफ॒फकफरै  सननननन Report for the year ended 41 March 2001
| 7.10 Short realisation of tax |

“

Incorrect assessment of sales tax resulted in’ short-realisation of kx)
amounting to Rs.0,89 lakh and penalty of 1१४. | ,34 lakh |

||

7.10.1 The Manipur Sales Tax Act, 1990, provides that every dealer liable tg
pay tax under any of the earlier laws, shall be Hable to pay lax under this Act on
all sales or purchases of goods, The Act, further provides that if any dealer
conceals the particulars 01 deliberately furnishes inaccurate particulars, the
Commissioner may direct that such dealer shall pay by way of penalty, in addition
10 the tax payable, a sum not exceeding one and half times of that amount

7.10.2 Test check of assessment records of the Commissioner of Taxes,
Manipur, Imphal revealed (August - September 2000) that three Imphal based
dealers dealing with two categories of goods taxable at the rate of 7 per cent and
10 per cent, had shown net turnover amounting Rs.37.24 lakh (during June 1995
to March 2000) as taxable at the rate of 7 per cent only, in their returns and were
assessed (during October 1995 to April 2000) accordingly, But from the goods
movement ledgers (Form ST 49) of the dealers it was noticed that the dealers had
purchased goods worth Rs.2.09 lakh only, taxable at 7 per cent.

7.10.3 Thus, net turnover of Rs.35.15 lakh (Rs.37.24 lakh — Rs.2.09 lakh)
though taxable at the rate of 10 per cent was assessed at the rate of 7 per cent,
This had resulted in short realisation of tax amounting Rs.1.05 lakh and attracted
penalty of Rs.1.58 lakh.

7.10.4 On this being pointed out in audit, the Department accepted the
objection and issued demand notices (June 2001). The Department subsequently
reported (August 2001) that one of the three dealers had paid reassessed tax of
Rs.0.16 lakh and the other two dealers have not paid the reassessed tax of Rs.0.89
lakh and penalty of Rs.1.34 lakh.

7.10.5 The matter was reported to Government (July 2001) , reply had not
been received (December 2001).

ee el on the part of appropriate authority led to non-realisation of sales
tax to the tune of Rs.81.51 lakh

7.11.1 The Manipur Sales Tax Act, 1990 and Rules framed thereunder,
provides that where a dealer is in default, the Commissioner may direct that, in
addition to the amount due, a sum not exceeding that amount shall be recovered
from the defaulter by way of penalty, and may order that the amount due shall be
recoverable as an arrear of land revenue and may proceed to realise the amount
due as such, and the appropriate Assessing Authority shall issue to the Collector a
recovery certificate.

7.11.2 Test-check of records of the Commissioner of Taxes, Manipur, Imphal
revealed (August-September 2000) that 141 dealers in 6 Sales Tax Zones/Offices
Were in default in payment of sales tax amounting to Rs.81.51 lakh from
September 1986 to December 1999. Moreover, action to recover the due as arrear
of land revenue was not taken except in one case for Rs.6.51 lakh.

711.3 The Department stated (August 2001 and November 2001) that out of
Rs.81.51 lakh, Rs.78.77 lakh had been collected from the defaulting dealers
leaving a balance of Rs.2.74 lakh which would be collected shortly.

7.11.4 The matter was reported to Government (July 2001); replies had not
been received (December 2001).

8.4.1 The accounts of the companies for every financial year are required to
be finalised within six months from the end of relevant financial year under
Section 166, 210, 230, 619 and 619-B of the Companies Act, 1956 read with
Section 19 of the Comptroller and Auditor General’s (Duties, Power and
Conditions of Service) Act, 1971. They are also to be laid before the Legislature
within nine months from the end of financial year. Similarly, in case of Statutory
corporations their accounts are finalised, audited and presented to the Legislature
as per provisions of their respective Acts.

8.4.2 However, as could be noticed from Appendix XXXIX, out of 14 working
PSUs (13 Government companies and one Statutory Corporation) none have

period from October 2000 to September 2001, 7 working Government companies
finalised 7 accounts for previous years.

8.9.2 Errors and omissions noticed in case of Government companies

~s

(a) — Manipur Plantation Crops Corporation Ltd. (1983-84)

(i) Expenditure (Rs.26.94 lakh) on the projects was understated by Rs.0.26
lakh due to wrong exhibition of the opening balance as Rs.12.33 lakh instead of
Rs.12.59 lakh as on 30.4.83.

(ii) Fixed Assets (Rs.8.02 lakh) was overstated by Rs.0.26 lakh due to
wrong accounting of the opening balances.

(iii) Cash and bank balance (Rs.22.58 lakh) and the nature of securities of the

secured loans (Rs.6.50 lakh) were not disclosed as required under the provision of
the Companies Act.

(b) — Manipur Electronics Development Corporation Ltd. (1994-95)

Fixed assets (Rs.66.37 lakh) include the value of land and building amounting to
Rs.15.24 lakh. The value of land has not been segregated from the total value of
land and buildings and depreciation has been worked out on the composite value

which resulted in understatement of profit with corresponding understatement of
assets in the balance sheet.

(c) Manipur Police Housing Corporation Ltd. (1992-93)

(i) Interest on Fixed Deposit (Rs.2.01 lakh) was understated by Rs.7.75
lakh due to its incorrect credit to Deposit for work instead of interest on Fixed
Deposit with corresponding overstatement of loans to that extent.

(ii) Loss was overstated by Rs.0.33 lakh during 1992-93 due to
overstatement of salary and allowances (Rs.64.40 lakh) by Rs.0.33 lakh being 3

months’ arrear dearness allowance which should have been accounted for under
prior period expenditure.

(iii) Inventories (Rs.159.94 lakh) was overstated by Rs.1.16 lakh (being the

value of CGI sheet) given on loan to different department of Government of
Manipur with corresponding understatement of loans and advances to that extent
due to wrong classification in the accounts under “Inventories” instead of “Loans
and Advances”.

(0. Manipur Spinning Mills Corporation Ltd. (1 981-82)

Pre-operative expenditure (Rs.26.35 lakh) was overstated by Rs.5.90 lakh (net)
due to short accountal of opening balance by Rs.4.46 lakh and excess accountal of
expenditure by Rs.10.36 lakh capitalised during the year.

(e)

Errors and omissions noticed in case of, ‘Statutory Corporations

| 8.10 Recommendations for Closure of PSUs

One Government company and one working Statutory corporation had been
incurring losses for five consecutive years (as per latest finalised accounts)
leading to net negative net worth. In view of poor turnover and continuous losses,
the Government may either improve performance of above one Government
company and one Statutory corporation or consider their closure.

[8.11 Response to Inspection Reports, Draft paras and reviews __| 8.11 Response to Inspection Reports, Draft paras and reviews

8.11.1 Audit observations noticed during audit and not settled on the spot are
communicated to the head of PSUs and concerned departments of the State
Government through Inspection Reports. The heads of PSUs are required to
furnish replies to the Inspection Reports through respective heads of departments
within a period of six weeks. Inspection Reports issued up to March 2001
pertaining to 13 PSUs disclosed that 182 paragraphs relating to 33 Inspection
Reports remained outstanding at the end of September 2001. Of these 85
paragraphs relating to 13 Inspection Reports had not been replied for more than 1
to 8 years. Department-wise break-up of Inspection Reports and Audit
Observations outstanding as on 30 September 2001 is given in Appendix XLIV.

8.11.2 Similarly, draft paragraphs and reviews on the working of PSUs are
forwarded to the Principal Secretary/Secretary of the administrative department
concerned demi-officially seeking confirmation of facts and figures and their
comments thereon within a period of six weeks. It was however observed that 2
draft paragraphs forwarded to the 2 departments during July to August 2001, as
detailed in Appendix XLV, had not been replied to so far.

8.11.3 It is recommended that (a) the Government should ensure that procedure
exists for action against the officials who failed to send replies to Inspection
Reports/draft paragraphs/reviews as per the prescribed time schedule, (b) action to
recover loss/outstanding advances/overpayment in a time bound schedule and (c)
revamping the system of responding to the audit observations.

8.13 Rural Electrification schemes 1318

Highlights

Due to non-payment of principal and interest on due dates penal interest of
Rs.1.05 crore was paid during 1999-2000.
(Paragraph 8.13.10)

There were shortfalls ranging between 27 and 89 per cent in electrifying
villages during 1995-96 to 1999-2000. No villages were electrified during
2000-01.

(Paragraphs 8.13.11 & 8.13.12)

Extremely low achievement of service connections (only 7 per cent) during
1995-96 to 2000-01 negated the economic viability of the RE schemes.

(Paragraph 8.13.15)

Non-completion of RE works in 227 villages resulted in locking up of
borrowed funds amounting to Rs.25.47 crore with consequential interest
liability of Rs.5.13 crore.

(Paragraph 8.13.19)

There was diversion of materials worth Rs.98.96 lakh from the RE schemes
to maintenance works.
(Paragraphs 8.13.21 & 8.13.22)

There was loss of Rs.1.21 crore due to theft of materials from RE works.

(Paragraph 8.13.23)

The Department proposed closure of 26 schemes (sanctioned between
February 1986 and March 1991) in June 1997 and failed to avail sanctioned
loan of Rs.5.51crore against them. As a result only 475 villages were

electrified against the target of 613.
(Paragraphs 8.13.27 & 8.13.28)

Due to slow progress/non-execution of special category schemes there was
locking up of Rs.9.74 crore resulting in fruitless liability of interest of Rs.1.31

crore per annum.
(Paragraphs 8.13.31 (९) & (d))



uit Report ft ie 3 I I Report for the year ended 31 March 2001

ere not monitored or evalua’
The RE schemes w' िविनिकिन नल
Introduction

8.13.1 Rural Electrification (RE) schemes were being implemented in the State
as State Plan schemes since 1969-70. The schemes were brought under the
purview of the Rural Electrification Corporation of India (REC) from 1977-78.
Since then, the schemes are funded concurrently under State Plan and through
loan assistance from the REC. The main objective of the schemes is to provide
socio-economic benefit to the rural population by electrification of villages and

intensification thereof.
Organisational set-up

8.13.2 The administrative control of the schemes rested with the State Power
Department. The Chief Engineer (Power) being the head of the Department was
responsible for technical guidance, planning, budgeting efc. He was assisted by
two Additional Chief Engineers (Electrical), one Additional Chief Engineer
(Civil) and two Superintending Engineers. The implementation of the schemes at
the district level was entrusted to the Executive Engineers (EEs) of seven?
Electricity Divisions. Apart from these Divisions, two Stores Divisions
(Electricity) were also engaged for procurement of stores required for Rural
Electrification (RE) works.

Scope of Audit

8.13.3 Records relating to implementation of the schemes for the period from
1995-96 to 2000-01 were test checked during April-June 2001 in the Offices of
the Chief Engineer, two Stores Divisions, four’ district level Divisions (out of
seven) and at the Finance Department.

the REC. Budgets were drawn up by the Department, after approval from the
State Planning Commission, on the basis of anticipated loans from the REC.

Loans from the REC

8.13.7 The REC loans are repayable over a period of 7 to 30 years with 12.5
per cent to 13.5 per cent interest per annum. The period of moratorium for
Tepayment of principal was 5 years which has been reduced to 2 years since 1999-
2000. The interest, however, was payable from the date of release of loans and
during the entire loan period. The terms and conditions of the loans stipulated a
rebate for repayment of principal and interest on due dates and in the event of
failure to do so a penal rate of compound interest was leviable.

8.13.9 11 was noticed from information furnished by the Finance Department
that the principal outstanding at the end of 2000-01 was Rs.131.16 crore. Ths,

REC came as reimbursement of expenditure incurred against the amount allocated
by the Planning Commission and if the expenditure is incurred at ‘an fag end of
the year, reimbursement is not received in the same year but in the following year.

Payment of penal interest

8.13.10 In absence of loan ledger, year-wise payments made against interest and
penal interest could not be verified. However, from the audited figures of the REC
for the year 1999-2000 it revealed that out of the total interest of Rs.28.45 crore
on the REC loan Government of Manipur repaid Rs.28.26 crore during 1999-2000
which included penal interest of Rs.1.05 crore. While admitting the fact the
Government stated (October 2001) the reasons for payment of penal interest was
due to suspensions of transaction imposed by the Reserve Bank of India. The fact

however remained that due to belated payments extra interest liability to the
extent of Rs.1.05 crore was incurred.

Abnormally low achievement of service connections

8.13.15 Test check of the records of four working Divisions (Chandel, Senapati,
Ukhrul and Tamenglong) associated with the RE works revealed that during
1995-96 to 2000-01 there was an overall shortfall in achievement of all the
principal items (as detailed in Appendix XLVI of works (HT line-44 per cent, LT
line-71 per cent, Transformers-40 per cent and Service connections-93 per cent).
The reasons for shortfall in achievement so far as HT & LT lines were concerned
the Department stated that targets could not be achieved due to non-availability of
line materials, poles, conductors, transformers etc. Each scheme, as sanctioned,
contemplates return by way of revenue from service connections (domestic,
commercial, industrial and agricultural) in a phased manner. Thus, the low
achievement of service connections (7 per cent) directly hampered the expected
Tevenue returns (ERR) and negated the economic viability of the RE schemes as
projected to the REC by the Department. As regards service connections the
Department has not assessed the loss of revenue.

Categories of schemes

8.13.16 RE schemes were implemented through Area schemes and Special
Category schemes. While Area schemes were adopted for laying of HT/LT lines
and setting up of distribution sub-stations for electrification of villages and
intensification thereof, Special Category schemes were taken up for infrastructure
development and other works of special nature like construction of 132 KV and
33 KV-Sub-stations and installation of high precision electro-mechanical/dynamic
and electric energy meters.

Area schemes

8.13.17 According to the 1991 Census, there were 2183 villages in the State of
Manipur with a total population of 10,79,711 spread over 8 districts. The overall
position of electrification of villages in Manipur (as reported to the REC by the
Department) up to 31 March 2001 was as under—

Audit Report for the year ended 3! March 2001

8.13.18 In terms of instructions issued (October 1997) by the Ministry of Power, _.
Government of India, a village is deemed to be electrified if electricity is used in
the inhabited locality, within the revenue boundary of the village, for any purpose
whatsoever. The RE schemes are not implemented properly. The shortcomings in
the implementation of RE schemes are discussed in the cases given below:

Inflated projection of electrified villages

8.13.19 Test check of records revealed that 163 villages were declared electrified
during 1995-96 without completing the extension of LT line around the villages
and without releasing any service connection, resulting in locking up of borrowed
funds of Rs.16.30 crore (taken @ Rs.10 lakh per village during 1995-96 as
intimated by the Department). Similarly, during 1996-97 to 1999-2000, 64
incomplete villages (under the jurisdiction of Electrical Divisions - Chandel,
Senapati, Ukhrul and Tamenglong) were declared electrified resulting in locking
up of funds of Rs.9.17 crore (calculated on the basis of the figures furnished by
the Department). The annual interest liability to the REC on this account works
out to Rs.5.13 crore at the rate of 12.5 per cent per annum.

8.13.20 The Department while admitting that the villages so declared were in
incomplete stage attributed (September 2001) non-completion of extension of LT
line due to receipt of materials at the fag end of the financial year. Thus, the
Department was not only inflating the projection of electrified villages but also
could not synchronise the receipt of material resulting in blocking of borrowed
funds. The non-completion also deprived the rural people of the intended benefits.

Diversion of materials from the RE schemes (Plan) to maintenance works
(Non-plan)

8.13.21 During 1998-99 to 2000-2001 the Department procured 3444 steel poles
(Rectangular poles :2444 nos. and Tubular poles :1000 nos.) and 398.160 KM of
ACSR‘ (weasel) conductor valued at Rs.2.07 crore from the RE funds (Plan)
through the Project Store Division, Porompat, Imphal. Out of the above ,1066
rectangular poles, 699 tubular poles and 82.197 KM of conductor. valued at
Rs.95.08 lakh were issued to and utilised for maintenance works (Non-plan) by
various Divisions. The effect of such diversion could neither be ascertained in
Audit nor intimated by the Department.

8.13.22 Further, one 63 KVA Transformer (valued Rs.0.33 lakh ) and 100 nos,
of rectangular poles (valued Rs.3.55 lakh) procured for the RE work/scheme by
the Chandel Division were issued to and utilised by Thoubal and Bishnupur
Divisions for maintenance works during 1995-96 and 1998-99. As a result of this
diversion, the electrification work of S. Lamphai village scheduled to be
completed by 1995-96 was delayed up to March 1998.

Short supply of poles

8.13.25 The Superintending Engineer (Purchase) placed (March 1999) a supply
order with M/s Fabrico (India) Pvt. Ltd., Meerut for supply of 6000 rectangular
steel poles (8 metre long) @ Rs.4222.53 per pole. According to the conditions of
supply, the poles were to be delivered within September 1999. However, the
supplier could supply 2000 poles only up to August 2001 and was paid Rs.110.56
lakh (including an advance of Rs.63.34 lakh). But against the supply of 2000
poles, only Rs.84.45 lakh was payable which resulted in undue financial aid of
Rs.26.11 lakh to the supplier. The short supply of the poles was one of the reasons
for shortfall in achievement of targets for electrification of villages during 1998-
99 and 1999-2000.

Closure of REC schemes

8.13.26 As per the procedure, closure format in respect of REC schemes which
(i) have achieved their physical and financial targets as envisaged in the schemes
or (ii) have completed their scheme period (5 years up to 1998-99 and 2 years
from 1999-2000) or (iii) have become prematurely sick/in-operative due to
various factors are required to be sent to the REC. ”

8.13.27 From 1977-78 to 2000-01 the REC sanctioned 138 schemes out of which
21 schemes had been closed on 22.6.92. Closure formats for the closed schemes
could not be furnished to Audit. Out of the remaining 117 schemes, 78 schemes
were due for closure out of which the Department forwarded (June 1997) closure
formats for 26 schemes (sanctioned between February 1986 and March 1991) to
the REC for approval of closure. An analysis of these closure formats revealed
that none of the targets were achieved and the schemes had to be closed due to
time overrun as detailed below—

8.13.31 Scrutiny of records revealed the following—

(a) For the Nambol 33 KV sub-station, out of the sanctioned amount of
Rs.2.14 crore, the REC released Rs.1.29 crore (March 2000). But no material had
been purchased and no electrical works had been started as the civil works were
incomplete as stated (June 2001) by the Executive Engineer, Sub-Station
Construction Division. The reason for non-completion of civil works was
attributed to non-finalisation of land acquisition process. It was, however, noticed
that Rs.31.25 lakh was spent during 2000-01 on payment of salaries of work
charged establishment and muster roll although there was no physical
achievement under the scheme (June 2001).

(b) Records also revealed that the Civil Division No.I, Imphal had incurred
expenditure of Rs. 7.11 lakh (December 2000 to March 2001) from the above
release for Nambol Sub-station on payment of land compensation (Rs.3 lakh) and
work-charged labour (Rs.4.11 lakh) for which there were no provisions in the
scheme, resulting in diversion of funds.

(c) Against the sanction of Rs.7.23 crore for the Kakching-Churachandpur
132 KV line, the REC released (March 2000) Rs.5.96 crore, out of which
expenditure of Rs.2.97 lakh only was incurred on the works up to March 2001 as
per the Power Department. Thus, due to slow progress of the works Rs.5.93 crore
remained locked up and resulted in interest liability of Rs.79.92 lakh (@13.5 per
cent) per annum. The Department stated (June 2001) that progress was slow due
to non-release of funds.

(d) For construction of Kakwa 33 KV, Kongba 132 KV and the Tipaimukh
132 KV Sub-stations against the sanctioned amount of Rs.19.06 crore (Rs.2.09
crore+ Rs.10.92 crore+Rs.6.05 crore respectively), Rs.3.81 crore (Rs.0.42 crore+
Rs.2.18 crore+Rs.1.21 crore) was released as first instalment of loan in March
2000 . No expenditure was, however, incurred on the projects up to March 2001.
This resulted in an extra financial burden of Rs.51.47 lakh per annum as interest
(@13.5 per cent).

8.13.32 Thus, slow progress of works/ non-execution of works affected the
augmentation of sub-station capacity, as envisaged, which would have enabled the
Department to extend electrification to villages and avoid overloading of the
existing sub-stations.

Monitoring and evaluation

8.13.33 The implementation of the RE schemes were not monitored or evaluated
at the State level. It was stated (April 2001) by the Department that monitoring of
RE schemes was carried out by the REC. However, no monitoring reports of the
REC could be furnished to Audit.

169


‘Audit Report for the year 2०८ 2 IS Report for the year ended 31 March 2001

Conclusion =

8.13.34 Rural Electrification schemes are being implemented in the State with
the funds borrowed from the Rural Electrification Corporation. Non-payment of
principal and interest thereon, on due dates, resulted in extra financial liability in
the form of interest/penal interest. In many cases loans drawn were found locked
up due to non-execution of schemes. Achievement in service connections was
extremely low thereby upsetting the expected revenue returns and economic
viability of the schemes. Concentrated efforts are needed to achieve the objective
of cent per cent rural electrification. The Department/Government may set target
after taking ground realities into consideration. Monitoring of execution of RE
schemes may be done at the Departmental level also. The Department/
Government may ensure that there is no diversion of materials from the RE
heads/Schemes.

SECTION ‘B’ - PARAGRAPHS
HOME DEPARTMENT

MANIPUR POLI

Failure of the Company in proper supervision of works in time led to an
extra expenditure of Rs.6.50 lakh

8.14.1 Two sub-works viz. (i) Construction of underground drainage system,
and (ii) Construction of athletic track, relating to the deposit work “Construction
of mini stadium at Thoubal Ningombam” on behalf of the Director of Youth
Affairs and Sports (YAS) were awarded (February 1989) by the Company to a
contractor at tendered amounts of Rs.1.47 lakh and Rs.2.45 lakh respectively.
Both the works were to be completed by 7 July 1989.

8.14.2 Test-check (November 2000) of records of the Company revealed that
on the strength of completion certificates issued by the concerned engineers of the
Company the contractor was paid Rs.3.73 lakh (Rs.1.45 lakh for item (i) and
Rs.2.28 lakh for item (ii) above) between May 1989 to September 1989. The
works, reported to have been completed, were inspected jointly (19 September
1989) by the Director of YAS along with the representative of the Company and
some defects in athletic tracks etc. were noticed and some additional works were
also suggested. These works were executed through another contractor.

8.14.3 In January 1997, a joint inspection was conducted on complaint about
water logging in the athletic track and surrounding ground. During inspection it
was found that laying of rubbles, stones etc. were not done as per the specification
by the former contractor. The contractor was asked by the Company to rectify the
defects, but after doing a little work he left the works (August 1997).

8.14.4 The Board of Directors of the Company in their 46" Quarterly Meeting
(14 January 1998) decided to complete the works including re-doing of the works,
cost for which would be met from the profits of the Company, and also ordered
for an enquiry against the delinquent engineers as well as blacklisting the
contractor. Accordingly, the Company completed the works of the athletic track
(July 1999) and the underground drainage (October 1999) departmentally at a cost
of Rs.6.50 lakh and handed over the same in November 2000. The Company had
blacklisted the contractor and initiated action against the delinquent officials,
result of which is awaited (May 2001).

8.14.5 Thus, failure of the Company in proper supervision of the works in time
led to an extra expenditure of Rs.6.50 lakh. On being pointed out, the Managing
Director stated (July 2001) that the extra expenditure incurred had to be recovered
from the contractor through arbitration which was already in process. Further

development is awaited (September 2001).

APPENDIX I
(Referred to in Paragraph 1.1; page 1)
Statement showing the structure of Government Accounts

Part-A - Government Accounts

I. Structure
The accounts of the State Government are kept in three parts (i) Consolidated Fund, (ii)
Contingency Fund and (iii) Public Account.

Part-I Consolidated Fund

All receipts of the State Government from revenues, loans and recoveries of loans go into the
Consolidated Fund of the State, constituted under Article 266(1) of the Constitution of India.
All expenditure of the Government is incurred from this Fund from which no amount can be
withdrawn without authorisation from the State Legislature. This part consists of two main
divisions, namely Revenue Account (Revenue receipts and Revenue expenditure) and Capital
Account (Capital receipts, Capital expenditure, Public Debt and Loans etc.).

Part-II Contingency Fund
The Contingency Fund in respect of Government of Manipur has not yet been created.

Part-III Public Account

Receipts and disbursement in respect of small savings, provident funds, deposits, reserve
funds, suspense, remittance efc., which do not form part of the Consolidated Fund, are
accounted for in the Public Account and are not subject to vote by tlie State Legislature.

II. Form of Annual Accounts

The accounts of the State Government are prepared in two volumes viz., the Finance
Accounts and the Appropriation Accounts. The Finance Accounts present the details of all
transactions pertaining to both receipts and expenditure under appropriate classification in the
Government accounts. The Appropriation Accounts, present the details of expenditure by the
State Government vis-a-vis the amounts authorised by the State Legislature in the budget
grants. Any expenditure in excess of the grants requires regularisation by the Legislature.
